Ehresmann connections in tangent categories
GEOFFREY CRUTTWELL, MARCELLO LANFRANCHI

Abstract
The theory of connections is at the very core of differential geometry. Discovered by Levi-Civita and Christoffel and later studied by Cartan, Koszul, and others, connections appear in their most general form under the name of Ehresmann connections. An Ehresmann connection consists of a splitting of the tangent bundle of a submersion into the vertical sub-bundle and a given horizontal distribution. In this paper, we generalize Ehresmann connection to a categorical setting called tangent categories . Initially introduced by Rosický in 1984 and later generalized by Cockett and the first author in 2014, tangent categories provide a categorical framework to study geometry that extends well beyond smooth manifolds, including algebraic geometry and non-commutative geometry. In this paper we introduce and study Ehresmann connections in the context of tangent categories. We give various equivalent formulations in term of full and abstract connections and prove that they generalize Koszul connections. We also define parallel transport and curvature for such connections, and prove the structural equation and the Bianchi identity for the curvature.



acknowledgements
.
We would like to thank JS Lemay and Geoff Vooys for the precious discussions on submersions and Richard Garner for advice and suggestions. The first author is supported by an NSERC Discovery Grant, while the second author is supported by the AFOSR under award number FA9550-24-1-0008.

1
Introduction

Connections are one of the most important concepts of differential geometry and they are at the very core of modern physics and mathematics. One of the most important applications in physics is found in the geometric formulation of general relativity, where the curvature of the Levi-Civita connection determines the gravitational field
[
21
]
. Gravity is not the only field that has a geometric interpretation. The field strengh of a gauge field, such as the electromagnetic or the bosonic fields of quantum chromodynamics, are also determined by the curvature form of a principal connection
[
8
]
.

Historically, the study of connections began at the end of the 19th century and in the early 20th century with the work of Christoffel and Levi-Civita. However, it was only with Cartan that it became an independent theory, separate from Riemannian geometry. Cartan’s goal to generalize Klein’s famous Erlangen program culminated with the discovery of Cartan geometries and Cartan connections. A few years later, Kozsul shed new light on the subject by interpreting connections as differential operators, in opposition to Cartan’s definition in terms of Lie algebra-valued differential forms.

Later, Ehresmann generalized Cartan’s approach to describe both Kozsul’s covariant derivatives and Cartan’s Lie algebra-valued differential forms. We suggest the reader consult
[
18
]
for a historical review of connections and Ehresmann’s contributions to the field.

Koszul connections were internalized and studied in the context of tangent categories in
[
3
]
by Cockett and the first author. In this paper, we do the same for Ehresmann connections.

tangent categories
.
Tangent categories offer a categorical framework for differential geometry. Introduced by Rosický in
[
19
]
and later generalized in
[
1
]
, a tangent category consists of a collection of objects, interpreted as models of geometric spaces, a collection of morphisms, intepreted as models of smooth functions, and an assignment,
\mathrm{T}
, which sends each object
M
to a new object
\mathrm{T}M
and each morphism
f\colon M\to N
to a new morphism
\mathrm{T}f\colon\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}N
.

The object
\mathrm{T}M
should be regarded as the space of tangent vectors of
M
, known as the
tangent bundle
of
M
, while
\mathrm{T}f
is interpreted as the smooth function which sends each tangent vector
u\in\mathrm{T}M
of
M
at
x
to the tangent vector
\mathsf{d}_{x}f(u)
, where
\mathsf{d}_{x}f
denotes the differential of
f
at
x
. The assignment
\mathrm{T}
satisfies the equations of a functor, that is,
\mathrm{T}
preserves identities and composition of morphisms.

A tangent category also comes with a family of natural transformations, called the
structural natural transformations
of the tangent structure. The first of these natural transformations is called the
projection

p_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to M
and is interpreted as the map that sends each tangent vector to its base point. The projection
p_{M}
comes with a section, that we call the
zero morphism

z_{M}\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
, which corresponds to the map that sends each point
x
of
M
to the zero tangent vector
0_{x}
at
x
. The zero morphism is the unit of the
sum morphism

s_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}M\to\mathrm{T}M
, where
\mathrm{T}_{2}M
denotes the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

of the projection along itself, and is interpreted as the space of pairs of tangent vectors
(u,v)
having the same base point. The sum
s_{M}
is regarded as the map that sends each pair
(u,v)
to
u+v
.

It is sometimes asked that
\mathrm{T}M
be equipped with a
negation
,
n_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}M
, which sends each tangent vector
u
to
-u
. In that case, we say that the tangent category
has negatives
.

Tangent structure has two more natural transformations, the
vertical lift
,
l_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}^{2}M
, where
\mathrm{T}^{2}M
is the tangent bundle of the tangent bundle of
M
, that is,
\mathrm{T}^{2}M=\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M
, and the
canonical flip

c_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}M\to\mathrm{T}^{2}M
. The vertical lift is used to construct a morphism

\displaystyle\xi_{M}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(l_{M}\times z_{\mathrm{T}M})\mathrm{T}s_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}M\to\mathrm{T}^{2}M

which makes the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}p_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}p_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

into a pullback. This universal condition is interpreted as follows: each fibre
\mathrm{T}_{x}M\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=p^{-1}(x)
of the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}M
of
M
is
linear
, that is, the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}_{x}M
of
\mathrm{T}_{x}M
is isomorphic to the Cartesian product
\mathrm{T}_{x}M\times\mathrm{T}_{x}M
. This linearity condition is precisely the condition enjoyed by Euclidean spaces, that are
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for which
\mathrm{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This expresses the idea that an object in a tangent category is a
locally linear
space and a morphism is a
locally linear
function.

Finally, the canonical flip,
c_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}M\to\mathrm{T}^{2}M
, is an isomorphism of the double tangent bundle, which encodes the symmetry of the Hessian matrix, explicitly, that the order in which two sequential partial derivatives
\partial_{i}
,
\partial_{j}
are taken does not matter.

The archetypal example is the category of finitely-dimensional smooth manifolds with the usual notion of the tangent bundle functor. However, tangent categories go well beyond smooth manifolds. In
[
6
]
, the category of affine schemes was shown to carry a tangent structure which captures some key aspects of algebraic geometry. This construction was later extended in
[
10
]
to algebras over an arbitrary algebraic operad. Convenient manifolds and
C^{\infty}
-rings also form tangent categories as well as the subcategory of microlinear objects of a model of Synthetic Differential Geometry (SDG) and every Cartesian Differential Category (CDC)
[
1
]
.

Numerous concepts of differential geometry have already been successfully extended to tangent categories. Vector fields, Euclidean spaces, vector bundles, Kozsul connections, and ordinary differential equations are only some of the concepts of differential geometry that have been internalized to tangent categories. In this paper, we add to the list Ehresmann connections.

submersions
.
In their most general version, Ehresmann connections are defined on submersions. A submersion, in differential geometry, consists of a smooth function
q\colon E\to M
, whose differential
\mathsf{d}_{y}q
at each point
y\in E
has maximal rank, that is, for every tangent vector
v\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M
of
M
at
x=q(y)
, there exists a tangent vector
u\in\mathrm{T}_{y}E
of
E
at
y
such that
\mathsf{d}_{y}q(u)=v
.

A submersion in a tangent category (Definition
2.29
) consists of a morphism
q\colon E\to M
, for which (1) the pullback
\mathrm{F}q
of
q
along the projection
p_{M}
exists, and (2) the unique morphism
\pi_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{F}q
which makes the following diagram commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

is a regular epimorphism in the appropriate category. The morphism
\pi_{q}
is called the
horizontal descent
of
q
and
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
is the
Finsler bundle
of
q
(Definition
2.20
). The pullback
\mathrm{F}q
can be regarded as the space of triples
(x,u,y)
formed by a point
x
of
M
, a tangent vector
u\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M
of
M
at
x
, and an element
y\in E
such that
q(y)=x
; the morphism
\pi_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{F}q
sends each tangent vector
v\in\mathrm{T}_{y}E
of
E
at
y
to the triple
(q(y),\mathsf{d}_{y}v,y)
. Thus, requiring
\pi_{q}
be regular epi is to say that for every triple
(x,u,y)
of
\mathrm{F}q
there exists a tangent vector
v\in\mathrm{T}_{y}E
of
E
at
y
such that
\mathsf{d}_{y}v=u
, which is precisely to say that
q
is a submersion. We will review this concept in Section
2.4
.

tangent display maps
.
Although not immediately apparent from their definition, submersions in differential geometry exhibit good behaviour with respect to two important operations: the tangent bundle functor and pullbacks along arbitrary maps. In particular, the pullback of a submersion along a smooth function exists and is still a submersion. From a tangent categorical perspective, stability under pullbacks plays a crucial role in the study of structures and properties of differential geometry. However, it is also a subtle problem to establish whether or not a certain morphism admits all pullbacks and whether or not those pullbacks are also preserved by the tangent bundle functor. In particular, pullbacks in the category of smooth manifolds are notoriously ill-behaved: one cannot assume their existence in full generality, and even when they exist, one cannot expect them to be preserved by the tangent bundle functor.

In previous work
[
7
]
, we tackled this issue by introducing a new class of maps that we called
tangent display maps
(Definition
2.1
). A tangent display map consists of a morphism
q\colon E\to M
satisfying the following property. For every
m,n\geq 0
, the pullback of
\mathrm{T}^{n}q\colon\mathrm{T}^{n}E\to\mathrm{T}^{n}M
along any morphism
f\colon N\to\mathrm{T}^{n}M
exists and is preserved by all iterates
\mathrm{T}^{m}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathrm{T}\circ\dots\circ\mathrm{T}
of the tangent bundle functor.
In
[
7
, Theorem 2.31]
, we shown that in the category of smooth manifolds, tangent display maps are equivalent to submersions.

However, on the one hand it is clear to us that tangent display maps are far too general to represent a good definition of submersion in a tangent category. On the other hand, submersions in the context of tangent categories do not need to admit pullbacks along arbitrary maps. Thus, we only consider submersions whose underlying map is also tangent display, that is, for us a submersion is a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
whose horizontal descent
\pi_{q}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}E
is regular epi in the appropriate category (Definition
2.29
).

the vertical and the finsler bundle
.
One of the key properties of a tangent display map is that it has associated
vertical
and
Finsler
bundles. In differential geometry the vertical bundle of a map
q\colon E\to M
consists of the vector sub-bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
of the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
of
E
, whose local fibre
\mathrm{V}_{y}q
at a point
y
of
E
is the kernel
\ker\mathsf{d}_{y}q
of the differential of the map
q
at
y
.

If you imagine to move within the space
E
, you can also imagine to move
vertically
with respect to the map
q
, that is, moving in a such a way to leave the position projected down to the base space
M
by
q
, unchanged. The tangent vectors that generate these vertical degrees of freedom are precisely those tangent vectors forming the vertical bundle of
q
.

In a tangent category, the
vertical bundle
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
(Definition
2.14
) is obtained by pulling back
\mathrm{T}q
along the zero morphism
z_{M}\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

The vertical bundle is then defined as the bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
obtained by composing
\iota_{q}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to\mathrm{T}E
with
p_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
. We will show that the vertical bundle carries the structure of a differential bundle, the tangent categorical-analogue of a vector bundle.

While the vertical vectors capture the vertical movements within the space
E
, there is no canonical
horizontal
direction, that is, a direction
parallel
to the base space that leaves the vertical component unchanged. An Ehresmann connection provides precisely such notion. In order to define an Ehresmann connection, one needs the Finsler bundle. As mentioned above, the Finsler bundle appears in the definition of a submersion.

In a tangent category, the Finsler bundle of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
(Definition
2.20
) is constructed by pulling back
q
along the projection
p_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to M
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

The pullback projection
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
comes equipped with the structure of a differential bundle, that we call, the Finsler bundle of
q
. As mentioned earlier, the Finsler bundle should be regarded as the bundle containing pairs
(y,u)
formed by a point
y
of
E
together with a tangent vector
u
of
M
at
x=q(y)
.

ehresmann connections
.
In differential geometry, an Ehresmann connection on a submersion
q\colon E\to M
consists of a choice of a sub-bundle
\mathrm{H}q\to E
of
p_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
called the
horizontal bundle
which is (1) isomorphic to the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
and (2) is in direct sum with the vertical bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
, explicitly,
\mathrm{H}_{y}q\subseteq\mathrm{T}_{y}E
and at each point
y
of
E
, the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{y}E
is equal to the direct sum
\mathrm{V}_{y}q\oplus\mathrm{H}_{y}q
.

An Ehresmann connection thus provides a system of coordinates for the total space
E
of
q\colon E\to M
, which allows one to write every tangent vector
u
of
E
uniquely as the sum of a vertical vector
u_{\mathrm{V}}
, that is, a vector belonging to the vertical bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
, and a horizontal vector
u_{\mathrm{H}}
, which is a vector of the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
once embedded in the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
.

To understand this notion in an arbitrary tangent category, it is useful to introduce the fundamental short exact sequence of a submersion.

the fundamental short exact sequence
.
Every submersion
q\colon E\to M
in a tangent category defines a short exact sequence

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}\to\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\xrightarrow{\iota_{q}}\mathbf{p}_{E}\xrightarrow{\pi_{q}}\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\to\mathbf{0}

that we call the
fundamental short exact sequence
of
q
, where
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
is the vertical bundle of
q
and
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
denotes the Finsler bundle.

The first major contribution of this paper is to define an
Ehresmann connection
(Definition
3.16
) on a submersion
q\colon E\to M
in a tangent category as a
splitting
of the fundamental short exact sequence of
q
(Theorem
3.21
). In particular, with an Ehresmann connection,
\mathrm{T}E
becomes (isomorphic to) the fibred biproduct
\mathrm{V}q\oplus_{E}\mathrm{F}q
of the vertical bundle and the Finsler bundle.

full and abstract connections
.
Our definition of an Ehresmann connection decomposes into two parts: a
vertical
and a
horizontal connection
(Definitions
3.1
and
3.8
). However, using the splitting lemma, we show that, in a tangent category with negatives, one determines necessarily the other one (Theorem
3.25
).

A different approach is taken by full connections. A
full vertical connection
(Definition
3.27
) is a vertical connection
\mathrm{R}
subject to an extra axiom
[FVC]
. This extra axiom suffices to uniquely reconstruct the horizontal connection which completes
\mathrm{R}
into an Ehresmann connection (Theorem
3.33
).

Similarly, a
full horizontal connection
(Definition
3.29
) is a horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}
subject to an extra axiom
[FHC]
. Every full horizontal connection admits a unique vertical connection which completes
\mathrm{H}
into an Ehresmann connection (Theorem
3.33
).

Vertical, horizontal, and Ehresmann connections can also be equivalently formulated in terms of connection forms (Definitions
3.3
,
3.10
,
3.17
). The correspondences between connections and their forms suggests a new generalization to Ehresmann connections, that we call abstract connections.

An
abstract vertical connection
is a linear idempotent
\phi
of the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
, subject to some properties (Definition
3.34
). The novelty in this definition is that it does not rely on the vertical bundle and therefore, it can be defined on maps which are not necessarily tangent display. However, Theorem
3.39
shows that a splitting of an abstract vertical connection is equivalent to a
choice
of the vertical bundle (the vertical bundle only being defined up to a unique isomorphism) together with a full vertical connection on it.

An
abstract horizontal connection
is a linear idempotent
\psi
of the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
, subject to some properties (Definition
3.42
). Again, this definition does not require the Finsler bundle. However, as with abstract vertical connections, Theorem
3.46
proves that a splitting of an abstract horizontal connection gives a
choice
of the Finsler bundle together with a full horizontal connection on it.

This proves an equivalence between full, split abstract, and Ehresmann connections (Theorem
3.47
).

koszul connections
.
Koszul connections on differential bundles have already been introduced in tangent categories in
[
3
]
by Cockett and the first author. Differential bundles are the tangent-categorical analogue of a vector bundle, that is, a fibre bundle
q\colon E\to M
whose local fibres carry the structure of a vector space. In fact, MacAdam and Burke proved that differential bundles in the category of finitely-dimensional smooth manifolds are equivalent to vector bundles
[
16
]
.

As Theorem
3.55
proves, Koszul connections on differential bundles turn out to be a special type of Ehresmann connections:
linear
Ehresmann connections. We also show an interesting alternative point of view: a Koszul connection on a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
can be seen simply as an Ehresmann connection on
q
when
q
is regarded as a morphism in the tangent category of differential bundles (Theorem
3.57
).

parallel transport and curvature
.
After spending Sections
4
and
5
to define
vertical
,
Finsler
, and
horizontal vector fields
(Definitions
4.1
,
4.8
,and
4.12
) and the notions of
distributions
and
involutivity
(Definitions
5.1
and
5.3
), we show that Ehresmann connections in a tangent category define
parallel transport
(assuming the tangent category has a suitable
curve object
) and carry a notion of
curvature
(Section
6
). We end by proving the structural equation for the curvature and the Bianchi identity (Theorems
6.13
and
6.16
).

related work
.
In
[
14
]
, Lemay and Vooys introduced some important classes of maps in a tangent category, including submersions. As aknowledged in their paper, we independently discovered the definition of submersion while working on the story of this paper, including the fundamental short exact sequence which, in their paper is called the
relative cotangent sequence
. However, Lemay and Vooys spent much more time in working out a full theory of submersions. We suggest the reader consult their paper for full details on this concept.

Lemay and Vooys also considered
linear
sections of the horizontal descent
[
14
, Section 8.3]
, which we call, horizontal connections, however, they did not focus of the geometric meaning of these linear sections.

They also decided to not consider tangent display maps as we do, and instead require the existence of tangent pullbacks as needed. In particular, they use the notions of
0
-carrable
and
p
-carrable
, which respectively means, that a map admits tangent pullbacks along the zero
z_{M}\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
and the projection
p_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to M
, respectively. This allows them to define the vertical and the Finsler bundle without the need of other pullbacks. The downside of this choice is the lost of stability under pullbacks: if
q
is
0
-carrable, this might not be the case for the pullback of
\mathrm{T}q
along
z_{M}
.

They also call the Finsler bundle,
horizontal
bundle and use different notations which are closer to the ones adopted in Algebraic geometry. Our naming convention avoids confusion with the horizontal distribution, which is the Finsler bundle once is embedded in the tangent bundle via the horizontal connection.

significance
.
Internalizing Ehresmann connections in tangent categories is not a mere exercise of generalization of an existing concept. Since 2014 (
[
1
]
), there has been a lot of effort to rebuild the familiar constructions of differential geometry within the setting of tangent categories (
[
4
,
5
,
17
]
). Conceptually, this program aims to upgrade tangent categories from an abstraction of the tangent bundle functor to a full theory of geometry, which provides a rich setting to support “doing geometry” in its own right, without committing in advance to any particular notion of geometric space.

At the same time, there has been a complementary drive to produce new examples of tangent categories to capture geometric worlds beyond smooth manifolds. Algebraic geometry as well as other geometric theories such as non-commutative geometry took great inspiration from differential geometry. In this spirit, the emergence of tangent categories built from (affine) schemes (
[
6
]
), and later from operadic affine schemes (
[
10
]
), makes a strong statement: what looks like quite different forms of geometry can all be understood as models of the same tangent-categorical theory. Differential, algebraic, and operadic geometry then appear not as unrelated subjects, but as different models of a shared abstract structure.

This justifies our effort to construct a theory of Ehresmann connections for tangent categories. Instead of being a concept of differential geometry, it now becomes a subtheory of tangent category theory that will reflect across the different models.

A further advantage of working categorically is that it makes the structural dependencies of the constructions particularly transparent: one can see exactly which axioms and which bits of structure are doing the work, and therefore what really needs to be assumed to obtain a given result.

This clarity becomes especially visible in three parts of the paper. First, the paper introduces abstract Ehresmann connections as certain linear idempotents on the tangent bundle, subject to compatibility conditions (Section
3.6
). Framed this way, connections are not restricted to the classical context of submersions; they can be defined over arbitrary maps. Within this enlarged notion, the “split” abstract connections are shown to coincide precisely with ordinary connections, so the new definition both extends the classical picture and recovers it under the splitting condition (Theorem
3.47
).

Second, the paper shows that Koszul connections, which in differential geometry correspond to the usual notion of covariant derivatives, fit naturally into this framework (Theorem
3.57
). More precisely, Koszul connections are Ehresmann connections in the category of differential bundles, in the appropriate sense. Rather than being a parallel theory, Koszul connections become an application, and in a sense a consequence, of the general theory of Ehresmann connections once it is set up at the right level of abstraction.

Third, the paper introduces a categorical notion of curvature (Definition
6.3
). Here curvature is understood as measuring a particular failure: the connection does not behave as a morphism of connections over itself in the relevant categorical setting. This reframes a central geometric idea as an instance of a precise categorical phenomenon, relating the intuitive content of curvature to an obstruction to a structural condition.

upcoming work
.
One of the objectives of this project is to provide a foundational workground for a theory of
principal connections
in tangent categories. In differential geometry, a principal connection can be equivalently defined as an Ehresmann connection on a principal bundle that is equivariant with respect to the group action. This will be the subject of an upcoming paper, in which many of the definitions and results introduced and proved in this paper will play a crucial role.

conventions
.
In this paper, we adopt diagrammatic composition, that is, we shall denote by
fg
the composition of
f\colon A\to B
followed by
g\colon B\to C
.

2
The vertical and the Finsler bundles of a submersion

In this section, we recall the definition of a tangent display map and show that every tangent display map admits a vertical bundle and a Finsler bundle. We then introduce submersions and construct the associated fundamental exact sequence.

2.1
Tangent display maps

Here we recall the main definitions and results of
[
7
]
, beginning with the definition of a tangent display map.

Definition 2.1
.

A
tangent display map
in a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
consists of a morphism
q\colon E\to M
subject to the following property. For every
n\geq 0
and every
f\colon N\to\mathrm{T}^{n}M
, the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{n}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{n}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{n}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

of
\mathrm{T}^{n}q
along
f
exists and is a
tangent pullback
, that is, it is preserved by all iterates
\mathrm{T}^{m}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=T\circ\dots\circ\mathrm{T}
of the tangent bundle functor.

Example 2.2
.

Every category
\mathbb{X}
comes equipped with a trivial tangent structure, whose tangent bundle functor and structural natural transformations are the identity. In such a tangent category, a tangent display map is equivalent to a display map, namely, a map
q\colon E\to M
for which, for any
f\colon N\to M
, the pullback of
q
along
f
exists.

Example 2.3
.

As proved in
[
7
, Theorem 2.31]
, tangent display maps in the tangent category of finitely-dimensional smooth manifolds are equivalent to submersions.

Example 2.4
.

In the tangent category of affine schemes (see
[
6
]
), every morphism is a tangent display map, since the category of affine schemes admits all pullbacks and the tangent bundle functor preserves those.

The main benefit of working with tangent display maps is that they form the maximal
tangent display system
, which is a family of morphisms
\mathscr{D}
stable under tangent pullbacks and the tangent bundle functor.

Theorem 2.5

(
[
7
, Theorem 2.11]
)
.

Tangent display maps in a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
form the maximal tangent display system
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Furthermore,
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is closed under composition.

Since tangent display maps are stable under the tangent bundle functor, they also form a tangent sub-category
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of the arrow tangent category. Concretely, the objects of
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
are tangent display maps and morphisms, commutative squares between the tangent display maps.

Thanks to Theorem
2.5
, the (tangent) pullback of a tangent display map along a morphism is again a tangent display map. Since we will be working extensively with tangent pullbacks, we recall here a simple yet important property of tangent pullbacks which generalizes a well-known property of pullbacks.

Lemma 2.6

(
[
7
, Lemma 2.3]
)
.

Consider the following two commutative diagrams in any tangent category:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}

If the right and the outer squares of the first diagram are tangent pullbacks, so is the left square. Moreover, if the bottom and the outer squares of the second diagram are tangent pullbacks, so is the top square.

Next, we recall a key notion in tangent category theory: differential bundles, which generalize (smooth) vector bundles.

Definition 2.7

(
[
4
, Definition 2.3]
)
.

A
differential bundle
in a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
consists of a pair of objects
E
and
M
together with the following structural morphisms

\displaystyle q\colon E\to M

\displaystyle z_{q}\colon M\to E

\displaystyle s_{q}\colon E_{2}\to E

\displaystyle l_{q}\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E

respectively called, the
projection
, the
zero
, the
sum
morphism, and the
vertical lift
, where
E_{n}
denotes the
n
-fold pullback of
q
along itself, which is required to exist and be preserved by all iterates of the tangent bundle functor. The triple
(q,z_{q},s_{q})
forms an
additive bundle
, that is, the following conditions hold:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}}

where
\tau\colon A\times B\to B\times A
denotes the canonical symmetry. Furthermore:

The pair

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}

is an additive bundle morphism:

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{E}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}

The vertical lift satisfies the following universal property: the diagram

is a tangent pullback diagram, where
\xi_{q}\colon E_{2}\xrightarrow{\langle l_{q},z_{E}\rangle}\mathrm{T}E_{2}\xrightarrow{s_{q}}\mathrm{T}E
;

[4]

The vertical lift
l_{q}
is compatible with the vertical lift of the tangent bundle
l_{E}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}l_{q}}

A
display differential bundle
is a differential bundle whose projection is a tangent display map.

We denote a (display) differential bundle by
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
and the corresponding projection, zero morphism, sum morphism, and vertical lift by
q
,
z_{q}
,
s_{q}
, and
l_{q}
, respectively. When the symbol adopted for a differential bundle is decorated with a superscript or a lowerscript, e.g.,
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
, we use the same decorations for the structural morphisms, that is, the structural morphisms of
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
are denoted as follows:
q^{\prime}_{\bullet}
,
{z^{\prime}_{q}}_{\bullet}
,
{s^{\prime}_{q}}_{\bullet}
, and
{l^{\prime}_{q}}_{\bullet}
.

Example 2.8
.

In any tangent category, every object
M
canonically has two differential bundles over it:

\star

The trivial differential bundle
\mathbf{0}_{M}\colon M\to M
, whose structural morphisms
q
,
z_{q}
, and
s_{q}
are simply the identity on
M
and whose vertical lift coincides with the zero morphism
z_{M}\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
. Notice that, as the notation suggests,
\mathbf{0}_{M}
is the zero object of the category
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};M)
of differential bundles over
M
;

\star

The
tangent bundle

\mathbf{p}_{M}\colon TM\to M
.

Example 2.9
.

In the tangent category of smooth manifolds, differential bundles are precisely vector bundles
[
16
]
.

Example 2.10
.

In the tangent category of affine schemes, differential bundles correspond to modules over rings
[
6
, Prop. 4.15]
.

We shall now turn our attention on morphisms of differential bundles.

Definition 2.11

(
[
4
, Definition 2.3]
)
.

A
morphism of
(
display
)
differential bundles

(f,g)\colon\mathbf{q}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
from a (display) differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
to a (display) differential bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
consists of a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

between the projections. A morphism is
linear
provided that it preserves the lift; that is, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}^{\prime}}

commutes.

(Display) differential bundles together with linear morphisms form a tangent category denoted by
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
whose tangent bundle functor sends a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
to the differential bundle
\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}M
whose projection, zero, and sum morphisms are
\mathrm{T}q
,
\mathrm{T}z_{q}
, and
\mathrm{T}s_{q}
, respectively, and whose vertical lift is the morphism:

\displaystyle l_{\mathrm{T}q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}l_{q}}\mathrm{T}^{2}E\xrightarrow{c_{E}}\mathrm{T}^{2}E

Furthermore, we denote by
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};M)
the tangent category of differential bundles with fixed target
M
and linear morphisms, whose tangent bundle functor is defined as in the slice tangent category.

Convention 2.12
.

In the following, unless otherwise indicated, we assume the differential bundles of a tangent category to be display; we also refer to a display differential bundle simply as a differential bundle, and we stress
ordinary differential bundles
when we only consider differential bundles which are not necessarily display.

As proved in
[
7
, Corollaries 3.8 and 3.9]
, under mild conditions, every differential bundle is display, provided that tangent bundles are display.

2.2
The vertical bundle

In differential geometry, the vertical bundle of a submersion
q\colon E\to M
is the sub-bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
of the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
on
E
spanned by the
vertical tangent vectors
, which are those tangent vectors
v\in\mathrm{T}E
of
E
sent to zero by the differential of the projection
q
. In other words, the fibre
\mathrm{V}_{y}q
of
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
at
y\in E
is the kernel of the differential
\mathsf{d}_{y}q\colon\mathrm{T}_{y}E\to\mathrm{T}_{q(y)}M
.

An important property exhibited by tangent display maps is that they always admit a vertical bundle. To begin, let us consider a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
in a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. By the properties of the tangent display maps, the (tangent) pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

(2.1)

of
\mathrm{T}q
along the zero morphism exists. The vertical bundle of
q
is the map
q^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
obtained by composing
\iota_{q}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to\mathrm{T}E
with the projection
p_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
. We want to show that such a map
q^{\mathrm{V}}
carries the structure of a differential bundle.

One could directly construct the structural morphisms of this differential bundle. Instead, we follow a more structural approach that clarifies the origin of this differential bundle.

As already proved by Rosický in
[
19
]
, the slice category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M
, that is, the category whose objects are tangent display maps with target
M
and whose morphisms are commutative triangles, comes equipped with a canonical tangent structure. In this tangent category, known as the
slice tangent category
, the tangent bundle functor sends each
q\colon E\to M
to
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q\colon\mathrm{V}q\to M
defined by the pullback of Equation (
2.1
) (see also
[
7
, Section 3.3]
for details on this construction).

To begin, recall that a
lax tangent morphism

[
1
, Definition 2.7]
from a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to a tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
consists of a functor
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
together with a natural transformation
\alpha_{M}\colon F\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}^{\prime}FM
, natural in
M
, which is compatible with the tangent structures. Recall that, a
Cartesian tangent morphism

[
4
, Definition 4.16]
is a lax tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
whose underlying functor
F
preserves tangent pullbacks and whose distributive law is Cartesian, that is, for each morphism
f\colon M\to N
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, the naturality square of
f

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}FM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\mathrm{T}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}FN}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\mathrm{T}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{N}}

is a tangent pullback.

Lemma 2.13
.

The domain functor
\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which sends a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
to
E
and a morphism
f\colon q^{E}_{M}\to q^{\prime E^{\prime}}_{M}
to
f\colon E\to E^{\prime}
extends to a Cartesian tangent morphism with distributive law defined as follows:

\displaystyle\Pi(\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q)=\mathrm{V}q\xrightarrow{\iota_{q}}\mathrm{T}E=\mathrm{T}\Pi(q)

Proof.

Straightforward by using Lemma
2.6
.
∎

[
4
, Proposition 4.22]
establishes that every Cartesian morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
sends ordinary differential bundles to ordinary differential bundles. In particular, given an ordinary differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
,
\mathsf{DB}(F,\alpha)[\mathbf{q}]
is the ordinary differential bundle whose structural morphisms are defined as follows:

\displaystyle FE\xrightarrow{Fq}FM

\displaystyle FM\xrightarrow{Fz_{q}}FE

\displaystyle(FE)_{2}\xrightarrow{\langle F\pi_{1},F\pi_{2}\rangle}F(E_{2})\xrightarrow{Fs_{q}}FE

\displaystyle FE\xrightarrow{Fl_{q}}F\mathrm{T}E\xrightarrow{\alpha_{E}}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}FE

Therefore, by Lemma
2.13
, the domain functor
\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
induces a lax tangent morphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{DB}(\Pi,\iota)

\displaystyle\colon\mathsf{DB}((\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M)\to\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

from the category of differential bundles of the slice tangent category to the category of differential bundles of the base tangent category. Recall that in any tangent category, each tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{X}\colon\mathrm{T}X\to X
is a differential bundle (Example
2.8
). Therefore, given a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, the functor
\mathsf{DB}(\Pi,\iota)
sends the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{V}}_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q\to q
of
q
in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M
to a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
.

Definition 2.14
.

The
vertical bundle
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
in a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is the differential bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
image of the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{V}}_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q\to q
of
q
in the slice tangent category over
M
via the Cartesian tangent morphism
\mathsf{DB}(\Pi,\iota)
.

Example 2.15
.

The universal property enjoyed by the vertical lift
l_{q}
of a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
can be equivalently expressed by saying that the vertical bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
of
\mathbf{q}
is necessarily trivial, that is,
\mathrm{V}\mathbf{q}=E_{2}
and
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}=\pi_{1}\colon E_{2}\to E
. This property of differential bundles can be understood as being “locally linear”.

Concretely, the vertical bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
of a tangent display map is the differential bundle so defined:

​[Projection]:

The projection
q^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
is the morphism defined as follows:

\displaystyle q^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\xrightarrow{\iota_{q}}\mathrm{T}E\xrightarrow{p_{E}}E

​[Zero morphism]:

The zero morphism
z_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon E\to\mathrm{V}q
is the morphism defined as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

​[Sum morphism]:

The sum morphism
s_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}_{2}q\to\mathrm{V}q
is the morphism defined as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}_{2}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}\times\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{M}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{M}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

where
\mathrm{V}_{2}q
denotes the pullback of
q^{\mathrm{F}}
along itself and
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}_{2}q\to\mathrm{V}q
, the projection;

​[Vertical lift]:

The vertical lift
l_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to\mathrm{T}\mathrm{V}q
is the morphism defined as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}z_{M}}

Remark 2.16
.

Definition
2.14
introduces the vertical bundle of a tangent display map. However, this definition can be easily extended to any map for which the tangent pullback of Equation (
2.1
) exists. In
[
14
]
, those maps are called
0-carrable
. In our paper, we need this level of generality only twice. In those cases, we will simply say that a map
admits the vertical bundle
.

Remark 2.17
.

It is important to realize that
the
vertical bundle of a tangent display map is only defined up to a unique isomorphism, since it is defined through tangent pullbacks. Using our notation
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
for the vertical bundle, we are implicitly making a
choice
of the vertical bundle. In Section
3.6
, we will develop a theory of connections free from this choice.

The vertical bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
of a tangent display map can be regarded as a sub-bundle of the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
, as proved by the next lemma.

Lemma 2.18
.

The morphism
\iota_{q}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to\mathrm{T}E
is
tangent monic
, that is, for every
n\geq 0
,
\mathrm{T}^{n}\iota_{q}
is monic.

Proof.

For starters,
\iota_{q}
is the pullback of
z_{M}
along
\mathrm{T}q
. Thus, since monics are stable under pullbacks and
z_{M}
is a section of the projection
p_{M}
,
\iota_{q}
is necessarily monic. Furthermore, since
q
is tangent display, for each
n\geq 0
,
\mathrm{T}^{n}\iota_{q}
is the pullback of
\mathrm{T}^{n}z_{M}
along
\mathrm{T}^{n+1}q
. However, each
\mathrm{T}^{n}z_{M}
is a section of
\mathrm{T}^{n}p_{M}
. Thus, again
\mathrm{T}^{n}\iota_{q}
is the pullback of a monic and therefore a monic itself.
∎

We conclude this section with a technical result that will be useful later.

Lemma 2.19
.

There is a strong tangent morphism
(-)^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which sends each tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
to the vertical bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
of
q
.

Proof.

For starters, let us show that
(-)^{\mathrm{V}}
is functorial. Consider a morphism
(f,g)\colon q^{E}_{M}\to q^{\prime E^{\prime}}_{M^{\prime}}
of bundles, that is, a pair of morphisms
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
and
g\colon E\to E^{\prime}
such that
qf=gq^{\prime}
. Define
\mathrm{V}_{f}g
as the unique morphism satisfying the following equations.
\mathrm{V}_{f}g\mathrm{T}^{M^{\prime}}q^{\prime}=\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}qf
and
\mathrm{V}_{f}g\iota_{q^{\prime}}=\iota_{q}\mathrm{T}g
. We leave it to the reader to show that
(g,\mathrm{V}_{f}g)\colon\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime\mathrm{V}}
is a linear morphism of differential bundles. Finally, to define a tangent morphism, we need to define a distributive law
(\mathrm{T}q)^{\mathrm{V}}\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DB}}q^{\mathrm{V}}
between
(-)^{\mathrm{V}}
and the tangent bundle functors. Define
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E},\gamma^{\mathrm{V}}_{q})
where
\gamma^{\mathrm{V}}_{q}\colon\mathrm{V}\mathrm{T}q\to\mathrm{T}\mathrm{V}q
is the unique morphism such that
\gamma^{\mathrm{V}}_{q}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q=\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{T}M}(\mathrm{T}q)
and
\gamma^{\mathrm{V}}_{q}\mathrm{T}\iota_{q}=\iota_{\mathrm{T}q}c_{E}
. Since the canonical flip
c_{E}
is invertible, it is not hard to show that
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\gamma^{\mathrm{V}}_{q})
is in fact an isomorphism. We leave it to the reader to show the compatibility conditions between the distributive law and the tangent structures.
∎

2.3
The Finsler bundle

We now introduce another differential bundle associated to a tangent display map. We refer to this as the
Finsler bundle
of a tangent display map. This naming choice was inspired by the name in differential geometry given to the bundle
\mathrm{T}_{2}M\to M
, which is called the Finsler bundle
[
12
, Remark 4.1.1]
. Our definition generalizes this bundle to an arbitrary tangent display map.

In differential geometry, given a submersion
q\colon E\to M
we may define a vector bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
on
E
whose fibre
\mathrm{F}_{y}q
at each
y\in E
is the Cartesian product of the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{x}M
of
M
at
x\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q(y)
and the fibre
E_{x}
of
q
over
x
.

To generalize this to tangent categories, consider a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
in a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. The tangent pullback of
q
along the projection
p_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to M

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

(2.2)

exists. By
[
4
, Lemma 2.7]
, the tangent pullback of a differential bundle is still a differential bundle. Thus, since the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to M
is a differential bundle, the projection
q^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
also carries the structure of a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
.

Definition 2.20
.

The
Finsler bundle
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is the differential bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
, which is the tangent pullback of
\mathbf{p}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to M
along
q
.

Remark 2.21
.

As with the vertical bundle (see Remark
2.16
), also the definition of the Finsler bundle can be easily extended to every map
q\colon E\to M
for which the tangent pullback of Equation (
2.2
) exists. In
[
14
]
, those maps are called
p-carrable
. In our paper, we only make use of this level of generality once. In that case, we will simply say that a map
admits the Finsler bundle
. The theory of connections we are developing extends to the more general case, as long as the required tangent pullbacks exist. Our choice of using tangent display maps allows us not to worry about these pullbacks. We also point out that Lemay and Vooys in
[
14
]
call the Finsler bundle the horizontal bundle. However, this clashes with the usual notion of horizontal distribution, which is the embedding of the Finsler bundle in the tangent bundle given by the connection.

Remark 2.22
.

As mentioned in Remark
2.17
for the vertical bundle,
the
Finsler bundle of a tangent display map is only defined up to a unique isomorphism, since it is defined through tangent pullbacks. In using our notation
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
we are implicitly making a choice. In Section
3.6
, we will develop a theory of connections free from this choice.

Remark 2.23
.

Notice that, since we have assumed all the differential bundles are display, each tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{M}
is automatically a tangent display map. Therefore, every morphism
q\colon E\to M
admits the tangent pullback of
p_{M}
along
q
. In particular, assuming the tangent bundles of a tangent category being display is equivalent to requiring the existence of the Finsler bundle for every morphism of the category.

Concretely, the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
consists of the differential bundle so defined:

​[Projection]:

The projection of
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
is
q^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
;

​[Zero morphism]:

The zero morphism
z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon E\to\mathrm{F}q
is defined as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

​[Sum morphism]:

The sum morphism
s_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}_{2}q\to\mathrm{F}q
is defined as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}_{2}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\times\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

where
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{F}}
denotes the projection of the pullback of
q^{\mathrm{F}}
along itself;

​[Vertical lift]:

The vertical lift
l_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}\mathrm{F}q
is defined as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}p_{M}}

The Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
comes with an important morphism that will play a crucial role in the definition of a connection.

Definition 2.24
.

The
horizontal descent
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is the unique morphism
\pi_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{F}q
which makes the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

commutative.

Lemma 2.25
.

The horizontal descent of a tangent display map defines a linear morphism of differential bundles
\pi_{q}=(\mathsf{id}_{E},\pi_{q})\colon\mathbf{p}_{E}\to\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
.

Proof.

Follows by straightforward calculation.
∎

We conclude this section with a technical result that will be useful later.

Lemma 2.26
.

There is a strong tangent morphism
(-)^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which sends each tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
to the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
of
q
.

Proof.

For starters, let us show that
(-)^{\mathrm{F}}
is functorial. Consider a morphism
(f,g)\colon q^{E}_{M}\to q^{\prime E^{\prime}}_{M^{\prime}}
of bundles, that is, a pair of morphisms
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
and
g\colon E\to E^{\prime}
such that
qf=gq^{\prime}
. Define
\mathrm{F}_{f}g
as the unique morphism satisfying the following equations.
\mathrm{F}_{f}g\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q=\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\mathrm{T}f
and
\mathrm{F}_{f}gq^{\prime\mathrm{F}}=q^{\mathrm{F}}g
. We leave it to the reader to show that
(g,\mathrm{F}_{f}g)\colon\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime\mathrm{F}}
is a linear morphism of differential bundles. Finally, to define a tangent morphism, we need to define a distributive law
(\mathrm{T}q)^{\mathrm{F}}\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DB}}q^{\mathrm{F}}
between
(-)^{\mathrm{F}}
and the tangent bundle functors. Define
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E},\gamma^{\mathrm{F}}_{q})
where
\gamma^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}\colon\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}q\to\mathrm{T}\mathrm{F}q
is the unique morphism such that
\gamma^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q=\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}qc_{M}
and
\gamma^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}\mathrm{T}(q^{\mathrm{F}})=(\mathrm{T}q)^{\mathrm{F}}
. Since the canonical flip
c_{M}
is invertible, it is not hard to show that
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\gamma^{\mathrm{F}}_{q})
is in fact an isomorphism. We leave it to the reader to show the compatibility conditions between the distributive law and the tangent structures.
∎

2.4
Submersions and the fundamental short exact sequence

Lemma
2.18
proves that the vertical bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
of a tangent display map can be allegedly regarded as a sub-bundle of the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
, since
\iota_{q}
is tangent monic. One would expect the horizontal descent
\pi_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{F}q
to be an epimorphism and that the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
could be regarded as the cokernel of
\iota_{q}
. However, in general, this is not guaranteed.

In the context of differential geometry, the horizontal descent
\pi_{q}
of a smooth function
q
sends a tangent vector
v\in\mathrm{T}_{y}E
of
E
at
y
to the pair
(\mathsf{d}_{y}q(v),y)\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M\times E_{x}
, where
x=q(y)
. Therefore, asking that
\pi_{q}
is surjective is equivalent to saying that for every tangent vector
u\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M
of
M
at
x
and every
y\in E
such that
q(y)=x
, there exists a tangent vector
v\in\mathrm{T}_{y}E
such that
\mathsf{d}_{y}q(v)=u
. However, this is precisely what it means for
q
to be a submersion.

We start by recalling a result due to Lemay and Vooys, which shows that the vertical bundle is always the kernel of
\pi_{q}
.

Lemma 2.27

(
[
14
, Theorem 4.2.1]
)
.

The vertical bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is the kernel of the horizontal descent, explicitly, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}

is a pullback in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
.

Remark 2.28
.

The original statement of
[
14
, Theorem 4.2.1]
establishes that the vertical bundle is the equalizer in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of the horizontal descent
\pi_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{F}q
against the morphism:

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}E\xrightarrow{p_{E}}E\xrightarrow{z^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}}\mathrm{F}q

We decided to adopt the equivalent form in terms of a pullback.

We may now define the concept of submersions.

Definition 2.29
.

A
submersion
in a tangent category is a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
for which the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}

(2.3)

is a pushout in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
.

Remark 2.30
.

It is important to realize that in Definition
2.29
, the diagram
2.3
is required to be a pushout in the category of differential bundles and linear morphisms over
E
, not in the base tangent category.

For a submersion, the square Diagram
2.3
is both a pullback and a pushout in the category of differential bundles. Recall that in a
semi-additive category
1
1

1

In the literature, it is sometimes called,
additive
(see
[
15
]
). We will reserve this name to categories with biproducts, enriched over Abelian groups.
, which is a category with finite biproducts, such as
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
(
[
15
]
), a
short exact sequence
consists of a sequence
0\to A\xrightarrow{\iota}B\xrightarrow{\pi}C\to 0
, where
0
is the zero object, such that the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

is both a pullback and a pushout square.

Theorem 2.31
.

For a given tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
of a tangent category, the following are equivalent:

[1]

q
is a submersion;

[2]

The horizontal descent
\pi_{q}
of
q
is the cokernel of
\iota_{q}
;

[3]

The sequence of differential bundles

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}_{E}\to\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\xrightarrow{\iota_{q}}\mathbf{p}_{E}\xrightarrow{\pi_{q}}\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\to\mathbf{0}_{E}

is short exact in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
.

Definition 2.32
.

The
fundamental short exact sequence
of a submersion is the short exact sequence of differential bundles of Theorem
2.31
.

Remark 2.33
.

In
[
14
, Definition 4.2.2]
, the fundamental short exact sequence is called the
relative cotangent sequence
in agreement with the algebraic geometry literature.

One of the desirable properties of submersions in differential geometry is their stability under pullbacks and the tangent bundle functor. Unfortunately, in a general tangent category, there is no reason for a submersion, as per Definition
2.29
, to behave well with respect to these two operations. For this reason, we introduce a stronger concept.

Definition 2.34
.

A
tangent display submersion
consists of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
for which, for any integer
n\geq 0
,
\mathrm{T}^{n}q
is a submersion. Furthermore, for every morphism
f\colon M^{\prime}\to\mathrm{T}^{n}M
, the tangent pullback
q^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
of
\mathrm{T}^{n}q
along
f
is again a submersion.

By design, tangent display submersions form a tangent display system, and therefore, they form a
tangent fibration
(
[
7
, Proposition 3.11]
). We shall not explain the details of this important structure, since it is not of primary importance for the story of this paper. Instead, we refer to
[
4
, Section 5]
.

Proposition 2.35
.

Tangent display submersions in a tangent category form a tangent display system
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Furthermore, the codomain functor
\Pi\colon\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a tangent fibration.

Proof.

Consider a tangent display submersion
q\colon E\to M
. By definition,
\mathrm{T}q
is again a submersion. Furthermore, for any
n\geq 0
,
\mathrm{T}^{n}\mathrm{T}q=\mathrm{T}^{n+1}q
is a submersion and for every
f\colon M^{\prime}\to\mathrm{T}^{n+1}M
, the tangent pullback of
\mathrm{T}q
along
f
is again a submersion, since
q
is a tangent display submersion. This proves that
\mathrm{T}q
is in fact a tangent display submersion. Now, consider a morphism
f\colon M^{\prime}\to M
and the tangent pullback
q^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
of
q
along
f
. By assumption,
q^{\prime}
is a submersion. Furthermore, since
\mathrm{T}^{n}q^{\prime}
is the tangent pullback of
\mathrm{T}^{n}q^{\prime}
along
\mathrm{T}^{n}f
, and since
\mathrm{T}^{n}q^{\prime}
is a tangent display submersion,
\mathrm{T}^{n}q^{\prime}
is also a submersion. Finally, consider a morphism
g\colon M^{\prime\prime}\to M^{\prime}
and let
q^{\prime\prime}\colon E^{\prime\prime}\to M^{\prime\prime}
be the tangent pullback of
q^{\prime}
along
g
. However,
q^{\prime\prime}
is also the tangent pullback of
q
along
gf
. Thus,
q^{\prime\prime}
must be a submersion. This proves that
q^{\prime}
is a tangent display submersion and therefore, that
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is, in fact, a tangent display system. Finally, by invoking
[
7
, Proposition 3.11]
, which establishes that every tangent display system defines in fact a tangent fibration, we conclude that the codomain functor
\Pi\colon\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is in fact a tangent fibration.
∎

3
Ehresmann connections

In this section, we introduce the main character of this paper: Ehresmann connections in the context of tangent categories. An Ehresmann connection consists of two distinct components: a vertical connection and a horizontal connection, which together form a splitting of the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}E
of the total space
E
of a submersion
q\colon E\to M
. A vertical connection is a map that projects a tangent vector
v\in\mathrm{T}_{y}E
of
E
down to a vertical component
v_{\mathrm{V}}
. A horizontal connection is a map that sends a pair formed by a point
y
of
E
and a tangent vector
u
of
\mathrm{T}_{x}M
where
x=q(y)
to a tangent vector
v_{\mathrm{H}}\in\mathrm{T}_{y}E
of
E
at
y
.

We start by introducing these two notions separately; we then bring them together to define Ehresmann connections.

3.1
Vertical connections

A vertical connection on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
consists of a linear morphism
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
from the tangent bundle of the total space
E
of
q
to the vertical bundle of
q
which restricts to the identity on vertical vectors. The kernel of a vertical connection defines a sub-bundle of
\mathrm{T}E
in direct sum with the vertical sub-bundle which encodes the horizontal bundle of the connection. We may begin by introducing this definition in tangent categories.

Definition 3.1
.

A
vertical connection
on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
consists of a morphism
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
subject to the following conditions:

​[VC.1]:

The map
\mathrm{R}
is a retraction of
\iota_{q}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}

​[VC.2]:

The morphism
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathbf{p}_{E}\to\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
is a linear morphism of differential bundles over
E
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{V}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}

[VC.1]
requires that
\mathrm{R}
be a projection to the vertical sub-bundle of
\mathrm{T}E
which sends every vertical tangent vector to itself and
[VC.2]
requires that such a projection must be linear.

We denote by
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
the tangent category of pairs
(q,\mathrm{R})
formed by a tangent display map
q
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and a vertical connection
\mathrm{R}
on
q
, and commutative squares
(f,g)\colon q\to q^{\prime}
for morphisms, that is,
qf=gq^{\prime}
, compatible with the vertical connections as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{V}g}

The tangent bundle functor of
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
sends each
(q,\mathrm{R})
to
(\mathrm{T}q,\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{T}})
, where

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{T}}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}E\xrightarrow{c_{E}}\mathrm{T}^{2}E\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{R}}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{V}q\xrightarrow{\gamma_{q}}\mathrm{V}\mathrm{T}q

and
\gamma_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}\mathrm{V}q\to\mathrm{V}\mathrm{T}q
is the strong distributive law of the tangent morphism
(-)^{\mathrm{V}}
of Lemma
2.19
.

Lemma 3.2
.

If
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
is a vertical connection on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
, then:

[1]

The morphism
(p_{M},\mathrm{R})\colon\mathrm{T}q\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q
is a bundle morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

[2]

The morphism
(q,\mathrm{R})\colon\mathbf{p}_{E}\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q
is a bundle morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Proof.

To prove (i), we compute:

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \mathrm{R}\iota_{q}p_{E}q

(
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q=\iota_{q}p_{E}q
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \mathrm{R}q^{\mathrm{V}}q

(
q^{\mathrm{V}}=\iota_{q}p_{E}
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ p_{E}q

(
[VC.2]
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \mathrm{T}qp_{M}

(
p_{E}q=\mathrm{T}qp_{M}
)

However,
\mathrm{R}p^{\mathrm{V}}=p
, by
[VC.2]
, thus:

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q=\mathrm{R}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q=p_{E}q

To prove (ii), notice that, by the naturality of
p
,
\mathrm{R}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q=\mathrm{T}qp_{M}=p_{E}q
.
∎

Thanks to
[VC.1]
, a vertical connection
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
defines an idempotent
\phi_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathrm{R}\iota_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
on the tangent bundle, called the vertical connection form of
\mathrm{R}
. The next definition formalizes this construction.

Definition 3.3
.

A
vertical connection form
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is an endomorphism
\phi\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
of
\mathrm{T}E
subject to the following conditions:

​[VCF.1]:

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

​[VCF.2]:

The morphism
\phi\colon\mathbf{p}_{E}\to\mathbf{p}_{E}
is a linear endomorphism of differential bundles;

​[VCF.3]:

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

The next theorem shows that the information of a vertical connection is entirely retained in its vertical connection form.

Theorem 3.4
.

There is a bijective correspondence between vertical connections and vertical connection forms on a given tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
. Concretely, if
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
is a vertical connection on
q
, the morphism

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathrm{R}}\colon\mathrm{T}E\xrightarrow{\mathrm{R}}\mathrm{V}q\xrightarrow{\iota_{q}}\mathrm{T}E

is a vertical connection form on
q
. Conversely, if
\phi\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
is vertical connection form on a tangent display map
q
, the unique morphism
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
of
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
rendering the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}

commutative, is a vertical connection on
q
.

Proof.

For starters, suppose that
\mathrm{R}
is a vertical connection on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
and consider the morphism
\phi_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathrm{R}\iota_{q}
. Since
\mathrm{R}
is a retract of
\iota_{q}
, that is,
\iota_{q}\mathrm{R}=\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{V}q}
, we immediately prove that
\iota_{q}\phi_{\mathrm{R}}=\iota_{q}
, that is,
[VCF.1]
holds.
[VCF.2]
is also immediate, since, by
[VC.2]
,
\mathrm{R}
is a linear morphism of differential bundles and so is
\iota_{q}
, since, by construction,
\iota_{q}l_{E}=l_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\mathrm{T}\iota_{q}
. However, linear morphisms of differential bundles are stable under composition
[
3
, Prop. 2.7(i)]
; thus,
\phi_{\mathrm{R}}
is a linear morphism of differential bundles. Let us prove
[VCF.3]
:

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrm{T}q

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \mathrm{R}\iota_{q}\mathrm{T}q

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \mathrm{R}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}qz_{M}

(
\iota_{q}\mathrm{T}q=\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}qz_{M}
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ p_{E}qz_{M}

(
\text{Lemma}\penalty\ \ref{lemma:vertical-connections}.(b)
)

Conversely, consider a vertical connection form
\phi\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
of
q\colon E\to M
and let us prove that
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
is a vertical connection. First, let us prove that
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
is well-defined. For this, we will begin by proving that
\phi\pi_{q}=p_{E}z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
, by using the universal property that defines the Finsler bundle. Consider the following:

\displaystyle\phi\pi_{q}q^{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \phi p_{E}

(
\pi_{q}q^{\mathrm{F}}=p_{E}
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ p_{E}

(
[VCF.2]
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ p_{E}z_{E}p_{E}

(
z_{E}p_{E}=\mathsf{id}_{E}
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ p_{E}z_{E}\pi_{q}q^{\mathrm{F}}

(
\pi_{q}q^{\mathrm{F}}=p_{E}
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ p_{E}z_{E}\pi_{q}q^{\mathrm{F}}

(
z_{E}\pi_{q}=z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
)

Moreover, by using
[VCF.3]
, we can also compute the following:

\displaystyle\phi\pi_{q}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \phi\mathrm{T}q

(
\pi_{q}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q=\mathrm{T}q
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ p_{E}qz_{M}

(
[VCF.3]
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ p_{E}z_{E}\mathrm{T}q

(
\text{Naturality of }z
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ p_{E}z_{E}\pi_{q}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q

(
\mathrm{T}q=\pi_{q}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ p_{E}z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q

(
z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}=z_{E}\pi_{q}
)

Thus,
\phi\pi_{q}=p_{E}z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
. By Lemma
2.27
, the square diagram is a tangent pullback in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
, and by
[VCF.2]
,
\phi
is linear. Therefore,
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
is well-defined. Moreover,
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
is automatically linear, since the morphisms of
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
are linear, thus,
[VC.2]
holds. Moreover, by using that
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}\iota_{q}=\phi
and
[VCF.1]
, we compute
\iota_{q}\mathrm{R}_{\phi}\iota_{q}=\iota_{q}\phi=\iota_{q}
. However, since
\iota_{q}
is tangent monic by Lemma
2.18
, this implies that
\iota_{q}\mathrm{R}_{\phi}=\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{V}q}
, that is,
[VC.1]
holds. Finally, we want to show that this correspondence is bijective. Consider a vertical connection
\mathrm{R}
on
q
. Thus, the vertical connection
\mathrm{R}_{\phi_{\mathrm{R}}}
satisfies the equation
\mathrm{R}_{\phi_{\mathrm{R}}}\iota_{q}=\phi_{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{R}\iota_{q}
. However, since
\iota_{q}
is monic, this implies that
\mathrm{R}_{\phi_{\mathrm{R}}}=\mathrm{R}
. Conversely, consider a vertical connection form
\phi
on
q
. Thus, the morphism
\phi_{\mathrm{R}_{\phi}}
is equal to
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}\iota_{q}
. However,
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}\iota_{q}
, by construction, is equal to
\phi
. Thus,
\phi_{\mathrm{R}_{\phi}}=\phi
.
∎

One of the crucial properties of tangent display maps is stability under pullbacks. The next result shows that this also holds for vertical connections.

Proposition 3.5
.

If
q\colon E\to M
is a tangent display map,
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
a vertical connection on
q
, and
f\colon M^{\prime}\to M
is a morphism, the tangent display map
q^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
, pullback of
q
along
f

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

comes with a vertical connection
\mathrm{R}^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{T}E^{\prime}\to\mathrm{V}q^{\prime}
, defined as the unique morphism rendering the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
