\mathrm{T}^{n}\iota_{\phi}
is monic, since
\iota_{\phi}
is a section).

We denote by
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q=\mathrm{V}_{\phi}q\to E
this differential bundle to distinguish it from the other one, which is denoted by
\mathbf{q}_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}
, instead. For starters, since
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
is a morphism of bundles, the underlying projection
q_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}
of
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}_{\phi}
must satisfies
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}q^{\mathrm{V}}_{\phi}=p_{E}
. However,
p_{E}=\phi p_{E}=\mathrm{R}_{\phi}\iota_{\phi}p_{E}
, therefore,
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}q^{\mathrm{V}}_{\phi}=\mathrm{R}_{\phi}\iota_{\phi}p_{E}
, which implies that
q_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}=\iota_{\phi}p_{E}
, since
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
is epi being a retraction. Next, let
z_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}
,
s_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}
, and
l_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}
denote the structural morphisms of
\mathbf{q}_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}
, respectively and let
z_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
,
s_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
, and
l_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
denote the ones of
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
, respectively. Since
\iota_{\phi}
is linear, it satisfies the following equations:

\displaystyle z_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}\iota_{\phi}=z_{E}=z_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\iota_{\phi}

\displaystyle s_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}\iota_{\phi}=(\iota_{\phi}\times_{E}\iota_{\phi})s_{E}=s_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\iota_{\phi}

\displaystyle l_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}\mathrm{T}\iota_{q}=\iota_{q}l_{E}=l_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\mathrm{T}\iota_{q}

However, since
\iota_{\phi}
is tangent monic, these equations imply that the differential structures of
\mathbf{q}_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}
and of
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
must coincide.
∎

Theorem 3.39
.

For a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
equipped with a morphism
\phi\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
, the following are equivalent:

[1]

\phi
is a split abstract vertical connection of
q
;

[2]

There is a full vertical connection
\mathrm{R}
of
q
whose connection form is
\phi
;

[3]

There is a vertical connection
\mathrm{R}
of
q
which is the vertical component of an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
of
q
and whose connection form is
\phi
.

Proof.

The equivalence between
[2]
and
[3]
was already proven by Theorem
3.33
. It is only left to prove the equivalence between
[1]
and
[2]
. We begin by considering a split abstract vertical connection
\phi\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
on a map
q\colon E\to M
, with section-retraction pair
(\iota_{\phi},\mathrm{R}_{\phi})\colon\mathbf{q}_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}\to\mathbf{p}_{E}
. By Lemma
3.38
, the differential bundle
\mathbf{q}_{\phi}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}_{\phi}q\to E
is a choice of the vertical bundle of
q
. Consider the diagram of Equation (
3.2
). By
[AVC.3]
, the outer square is a tangent pullback and
(\iota_{\phi},\mathrm{R}_{\phi})
and
(z_{M},p_{M})
are section-retraction pairs. Therefore, by Lemma
3.37
, both the left and the right squares are tangent pullbacks.

Furthermore, since the left square of the above diagram is a tangent pullback,
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
satisfies
[FVC]
. Thus, to prove that
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
is a full vertical connection, it is only left to prove that
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
is, in fact, a vertical connection. To this end, we harness the correspondence of Theorem
3.4
between vertical connections and vertical connection forms and show that
\phi
is, in fact, a vertical connection form.
[VCF.1]
is a direct consequence of
\iota_{\phi}
being the section in the splitting of
\phi
, that is,
\iota_{\phi}\phi=\iota_{\phi}\mathrm{R}\iota_{\phi}=\iota_{\phi}
;
[VCF.2]
corresponds to
[AVC.2]
and
[AVC.3]
implies
[VCF.3]
. Thus,
\phi
is a vertical connection form and therefore,
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
is a full vertical connection, as expected.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
is a full vertical connection on
q
, then, by Theorem
3.4
, the associated vertical connection form
\phi_{\phi}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathrm{R}\iota_{q}
is a linear idempotent
\phi_{\mathrm{R}}\colon\mathbf{p}_{E}\to\mathbf{p}_{E}
of
E
. Furthermore, the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\mathrm{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

commutes and, by
[FVC]
and Equation (
2.1
) is the composition of two tangent pullback diagrams. Therefore,
\phi_{\mathrm{R}}
satisfies both
[AVC.1]
and
[AVC.2]
.
∎

Remark 3.40
.

Theorem
3.39
shows a correspondence between split abstract vertical connections, full vertical connections, and Ehresmann connections. However, this correspondence only works when the base map
q\colon E\to M
is a tangent display map. In fact, in order to construct the horizontal component
\mathrm{H}
of the associated Ehresmann connection, we make use of the existence of the Finsler bundle of
q
. A splitting of an abstract vertical connection on an arbitrary map
q
equips
q
with a notion of vertical bundle, which is implicit in a full vertical connection. However, it does not provide a notion for the Finsler bundle. It might accidentally happen that
q
does have a vertical bundle, but it does not admit a Finsler bundle. In that case, the correspondence between split abstract vertical connections and full vertical connections still exists, but full connections might fail to be completed into an Ehresmann connection. In that sense, both full and abstract vertical connections are a generalization of Ehresmann connections.

Full vertical connections on maps form a tangent category
\mathsf{fVC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, whose objects are pairs
(q,\mathrm{R})
formed by a map
q\colon E\to M
which admits a vertical bundle, that is, the tangent pullback of Equation (
2.1
) exists, and a full vertical connection
\mathrm{R}
on
q
. The morphisms and the tangent structure are defined as in
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Similarly, abstract vertical connections on maps form also a tangent category
\mathsf{AVC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, whose objects are pairs
(q,\phi)
formed by a map
q\colon E\to M
together with an abstract vertical connection
\phi
on
q
. The morphisms are morphisms of bundles that commute with the abstract vertical connections in the obvious way, and the tangent bundle functor sends a pair
(q,\phi)
to
(\mathrm{T}q,c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi c_{E})
. Split abstract vertical connections form a tangent sub-category of
\mathsf{AVC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
denoted by
\mathsf{sAVC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proposition 3.41
.

There is an equivalence of tangent categories:

\displaystyle\mathsf{fVC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\simeq\mathsf{sAVC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

Proof.

Theorem
3.39
gives a correspondence between full vertical connections and split abstract vertical connections. Using this correspondence we may define two functors, one that sends a full vertical connection
(q,\mathrm{R})
to the split abstract vertical connection
(q,\phi_{\mathrm{R}})
,
\phi_{\mathrm{R}}
being the associated vertical connection form of
\mathrm{R}
, and the second, that sends a split abstract vertical connection
(q,\phi)
with splitting
(\mathrm{V}_{\phi}q,\iota_{\phi},\mathrm{R}_{\phi})
to
(q,\mathrm{R}_{\phi})
. Starting from a full vertical connection
(q,\mathrm{R})
, one constructs the associated abstract vertical connection
(q,\phi_{\mathrm{R}})
. By choosing a splitting of
\phi_{\mathrm{R}}
, we define a full vertical connection
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
. However, since splittings are unique up to a unique isomorphism, there must be a (necessarily unique) linear isomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathrm{V}_{\phi}q\to\mathrm{V}q
such that
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}\varphi=\mathrm{R}
. Conversely, given a split abstract vertical connection
(q,\phi)
, by choosing a splitting of
\phi
, one obtains a full vertical connection
\mathrm{R}_{\phi}
. Thus, the associated connection form defines a split abstract vertical connection which is precisely equal to
\phi
. This proves the existence of an equivalence of categories. It is not hard to see that the tangent structures of
\mathsf{fVC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and of
\mathsf{sAVC}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
correspond via this equivalence.
∎

We may now define an abstract horizontal connection.

Definition 3.42
.

An
abstract horizontal connection
on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
consists of a morphism
\psi\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
subject to the following conditions:

​[AHC.1]:

\psi
is an idempotent, that is,
\psi\psi=\psi
;

​[AHC.2]:

\psi\colon\mathbf{p}_{E}\to\mathbf{p}_{E}
is a linear morphism of differential bundles, that is,
\psi p_{E}=p_{E}
and
\psi l_{E}=l_{E}\mathrm{T}\psi
;

​[AHC.3]:

The following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is a pushout diagram in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
.

An abstract horizontal connection
\psi\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M

splits
provided that the linear idempotent
\psi
of
E
splits.

Remark 3.43
.

In defining an abstract horizontal connection, we have already assumed the base map
q
to be tangent display, in contrast with Definition
3.34
in which
q
was not required to satisfy this condition. The reason for this choice lies in axiom
[AHC.3]
, in which we explicitly make use of the vertical bundle of
q
. One might only assume the existence of the vertical bundle instead of asking
q
to be tangent display (see Remark
2.16
). However, for simplicity, we have decided to assume that
q
satisfies the full display condition.

We would like show that splittings of abstract horizontal connections correspond to a
choice
of the Finsler bundle of
q
together with a full horizontal connection on
q
. However, in order to fully prove this correspondence, we need to require
q
to be a submersion. The reason for this assumption lies in the fact that the pushout diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}_{\psi}q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{\psi}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

defines the Finsler bundle
\mathrm{F}_{\psi}q\to E
of
q
only when
q
is a submersion. In fact, there is no reason to believe that, in general,
\mathrm{F}_{\psi}q\to E
would be the Finsler bundle of
q
when
q
fails to be a submersion. We start with a technical lemma, which is the dual of Lemma
3.37
.

Lemma 3.44
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a category. Consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

of
\mathbb{X}
, where
(s,r)\colon A\to B
and
(s^{\prime},r^{\prime})\colon A\to B^{\prime}
are two section-retraction pairs. Then, the outer square is a pushout if and only if the left and the right squares are both pushouts.

Proof.

Use the dual argument of Lemma
3.37
.
∎

Lemma 3.45
.

If
\psi\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
is a split abstract horizontal connection on a submersion
q\colon E\to M
and
(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\psi},\mathrm{H}_{\psi},\pi_{\psi})
is a splitting of
\psi
, then the differential bundle
\mathbf{q}_{\psi}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}_{\psi}q\to E
is a choice of the Finsler bundle of
q
.

Proof.

Consider a split abstract horizontal connection
\psi
on
q
with a linear splitting
(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\psi},\mathrm{H}_{\psi},\pi_{\psi})
. Since the splitting is in the category of differential bundles,
\mathbf{q}_{\psi}^{\mathrm{F}}
is already a differential bundles. We want to prove that
\mathbf{q}_{\psi}^{\mathrm{F}}
coincides with the Finsler bundle of
q
. Consider the following diagram in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}_{\psi}^{\mathrm{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\iota_{q}\pi_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{\psi}}

(3.3)

By
[AHC]
, the outer diagram is a pushout. Therefore, thanks to Lemma
3.44
, the inner square diagrams are also pushout diagrams. In particular, the left square diagram is a pushout in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
. However, since
q
is a submersion, the pushout of
q^{\mathrm{V}}
along
\iota_{q}
defines the Finsler bundle up to a unique isomorphism. Thus,
\mathbf{q}_{\psi}^{\mathrm{F}}
represents a choice of the Finsler bundle of
q
.
∎

Theorem 3.46
.

Given a submersion
q\colon E\to M
equipped with a morphism
\psi\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
, the following are equivalent:

[1]

\psi
is a split abstract horizontal connection on
q
;

[2]

There is a full horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}
whose horizontal form is
\psi
;

[3]

There is a horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}
which is the horizontal component of an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
on
q
and whose connection form is
\psi
.

Proof.

The equivalence between
[2]
and
[3]
was already proven by Theorem
3.33
. It is only left to prove the equivalence between
[1]
and
[2]
. To begin, consider an abstract horizontal connection
\psi\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{T}E
on the submersion
q
. Furthermore, let us assume that
\psi
splits, that is, there is a section-retraction pair
(\mathrm{H}_{\psi},\pi_{\psi})\colon\mathbf{q}_{\psi}^{\mathrm{F}}\to\mathbf{p}_{E}
. By Lemma
3.45
,
\mathbf{q}_{\psi}^{\mathrm{F}}
defines a choice of the Finsler bundle of
q
. Now, consider again the diagram of Equation (
3.3
). By Lemma
3.44
, the right square diagram is a pushout in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
, therefore,
\mathrm{H}_{\psi}
satisfies
[FHC]
. In order to prove that
\mathrm{H}_{\psi}
is full horizontal connection, it is only left to show that
\mathrm{H}_{\psi}
is a horizontal connection. To this end, we leverage the correspondence of Theorem
3.11
between horizontal connections and horizontal connection forms of submersions and show that
\psi
is in fact a horizontal connection form.
[HCF.1]
corresponds to
[AHC.1]
, since
\psi\pi_{\psi}=\pi_{\psi}\mathrm{H}_{\psi}\pi_{\psi}=\pi_{\psi}
; finally,
[HCF.2]
corresponds to
[AHC.2]
. Thus,
\psi
is a horizontal connection form and therefore,
\mathrm{H}_{\psi}
is a full horizontal connection.

Conversely, using Theorem
3.11
, it is immediate to see that the associated horizontal connection form of a full horizontal connection satisfies the axioms of a split abstract horizontal connection.
∎

We end this section by collecting in the next theorem all the equivalent forms of an Ehresmann connection that we explored.

Theorem 3.47
.

For a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
equipped with two maps
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
and
\mathrm{H}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}E
, the following are equivalent:

[1]

(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
is an Ehresmann connection on
q
;

[2]

q
is a submersion and
(\phi,\psi)
is an Ehresmann connection form on
q
; moreover,
\phi=\mathrm{R}\iota_{q}
and
\psi=\pi_{q}\mathrm{H}
;

[3]

q
is a submersion and
\mathrm{R}
is a full vertical connection on
q
;

[4]

q
is a submersion and
\mathrm{H}
is a full horizontal connection on
q
;

[5]

q
is a submersion and
\phi
is a split abstract vertical connection on
q
; moreover,
\phi=\mathrm{R}\iota_{q}
;

[6]

q
is a submersion and
\psi
is a split astract horizontal connection on
q
, moreover,
\psi=\pi_{q}\mathrm{H}
.

3.7
Linear vs Koszul connections

In
[
3
]
, Cockett and the first author introduced and studied connections on differential bundles in the context of tangent categories. In this section, we compare their work with our notion of Ehresmann connection. To distinguish the two, we will refer to their notion as a
Koszul connection
. To begin, we recall their definitions, starting from the notion of a vertical Koszul connection.

Definition 3.48

(
[
3
, Definition 3.2]
)
.

A
vertical Koszul connection
on a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}
consists of a morphism
\mathsf{K}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
, where
E
is the total space of
\mathbf{q}
, subject to the following conditions:

​[VKC.1]:

\mathsf{K}
is a retract of the vertical lift
l_{q}\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
, that is,
l_{q}\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{id}_{E}
;

​[VKC.2]:

(p_{M},\mathsf{K})\colon\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q}\to\mathbf{q}
is a linear morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathsf{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}

​[VKC.3]:

(q,\mathsf{K})\colon\mathbf{p}_{E}\to\mathbf{q}
is a linear morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathsf{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}

Next, we recall the notion of a horizontal Koszul connection. To this end, note that, for a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
, not only is the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to E
a differential bundle, but also the projection
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}M
onto
\mathrm{T}M
carries the structure of a differential bundle, since
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q
is a tangent pullback of
\mathbf{q}
and that differential bundles are stable under tangent pullbacks (
[
4
, Lemma 2.7]
). Furthermore,
\mathrm{T}q
also carries the structure of a differential bundle whose projection, zero, and sum morphisms are the image of the projection, zero, and sum morphisms of
q
along
\mathrm{T}
and the vertical lift is defined as follows:

\displaystyle l_{\mathrm{T}q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}l_{q}}\mathrm{T}^{2}E\xrightarrow{c_{E}}\mathrm{T}^{2}E

We may denote the differential bundles over
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q
and
\mathrm{T}q
, respectively as
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}\mathbf{q}
and
\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q}
.

Definition 3.49

(
[
3
, Definition 4.5]
)
.

A
horizontal Koszul connection
on a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}
consists of a morphism
\mathrm{H}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}E
, satisfying the following conditions:

​[HKC.1]:

\mathrm{H}
is a section of the horizontal descent
\pi_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{F}q
;

​[HKC.2]:

(\mathsf{id}_{E},\mathrm{H})\colon\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}\to\mathbf{p}_{E}
is a linear morphism of differential bundles:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{E}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathrm{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{E}}

​[HKC.3]:

(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\mathrm{H})\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}\mathbf{q}\to\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q}
is a linear morphism of differential bundles:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathrm{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}l_{q}c_{E}}

where
l_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
denotes the vertical lift of
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}\mathbf{q}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}M
.

Finally, we can recall the definition of a Koszul connection.

Definition 3.50

(
[
3
, Definition 5.2]
)
.

A
Koszul connection
on a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
consists of a pair
(\mathsf{K},\mathrm{H})
formed by a vertical Koszul connection
\mathsf{K}
of
\mathbf{q}
together with a horizontal Koszul connection
\mathrm{H}
of
\mathbf{q}
, subject to the following conditions:

​[KC.1]:

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}}

​[KC.2]:

The following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle\mathsf{K},p_{E}\rangle\xi_{q},\pi_{q}\mathrm{H}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{E}}

where
\xi_{q}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(l_{q},z_{E})\mathrm{T}s_{q}\colon E_{2}\to\mathrm{T}E

The universal property of the vertical lift of differential bundles requires that the vertical bundle of a differential bundle is necessarily the trivial bundle
\pi_{1}\colon E_{2}\to E
(see Example
2.15
). Using this property of differential bundles, Cockett and the first author in
[
3
, Section 3.35]
, realized that a vertical Koszul connection is equivalent to a so-called
Finsler connection
, that is, a map
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E_{2}
, which is a retraction of the map
\xi_{q}
and that satisfies some linearity conditions. We now recontextualize this result within the general theory of Ehresmann connections. We begin by defining the notions of linear vertical, horizontal, and Ehresmann connections. To this end, note that, using again the stability of differential bundles under the tangent bundle functor and tangent pullbacks, for a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}
, also the map
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q\colon\mathrm{V}q\to M
carries the structure of a differential bundle that we may denote by
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}\mathbf{q}
.

Definition 3.51
.

A
linear vertical connection
on a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
consists of a vertical connection
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
on
q
as per Definition
3.1
subject to the following condition:

​[VLC]:

The bundle morphism
(p_{M},\mathrm{R})\colon\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q}\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}\mathbf{q}
of Lemma
3.2
is linear

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}l_{q}c_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}\mathbf{q}}}

where
l_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}\mathbf{q}}
denotes the vertical lift of
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}\mathbf{q}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to M
.

Definition 3.52
.

A
linear horizontal connection
on a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
consists of a horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}E
on
q
as per Definition
3.8
, subject to the following condition:

​[HLC]:

The bundle morphism
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\mathrm{H})\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}\mathbf{q}\to\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q}
of Lemma
3.9
is linear

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathrm{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}l_{q}c_{E}}

where
l_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
denotes the vertical lift of
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}\mathbf{q}
.

Definition 3.53
.

A
linear Ehresmann connection
on a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
consists of an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
of the underlying tangent display map
q
, satisfying the further conditions:

​[VLC]:

The vertical connection
\mathrm{R}
is linear;

​[HLC]:

The horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}
is linear.

Thanks to the triviality of the vertical bundle of differential bundles (Example
2.15
), linear vertical connections become equivalent to vertical Koszul connections, as already proved by Cockett and Cruttwell. We recall here this result. For starters, recall that, by the universal property of the vertical lift
l_{q}
of a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}
, for each morphism
f\colon N\to\mathrm{T}E
satisfying
f\mathrm{T}q=f\mathrm{T}qp_{M}z_{M}
, there exists a unique morphism
\tilde{f}\colon N\to E_{2}
such that
\tilde{f}\xi_{q}=f
and
\tilde{f}\pi_{1}q=f\mathrm{T}qp_{M}
. This defines a morphism
\{f\}\colon N\to E
as
\tilde{f}\pi_{1}
. Details about this operation
\{-\}
can be found in
[
4
]
.

Proposition 3.54

(
[
3
, Theorem 3.40]
)
.

Consider a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
and a morphism
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
. The following are equivalent:

[1]

\mathrm{R}
is a linear vertical connection;

[2]

The unique morphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{\phi_{\mathrm{R}}\}_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E

induced by the universal property of the vertical lift
l_{q}
of
\mathbf{q}
, is a vertical Koszul connection, where
\phi_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathrm{R}\iota_{q}
.

Using this result, we can prove an equivalence between Koszul connections and linear Ehresmann connections.

Theorem 3.55
.

Consider a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
and two morphisms
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
and
\mathrm{H}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}E
. The following are equivalent:

[1]

(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
is a linear Ehresmann connection on
\mathbf{q}
;

[2]

(\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{R}},\mathrm{H})
is a Koszul connection on
\mathbf{q}
, where
\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{R}}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
is defined as in Proposition
3.54
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Proposition
3.54
since horizontal Koszul connections are exactly horizontal (Ehresmann) connections and the orthogonality conditions
[KC.1]
and
[KC.2]
, correspond directly to
[LC.1]
and
[LC.2]
,
∎

Now that we have clarified the relationship between Ehresmann connections and Koszul connections, we want to give a new perspective on Koszul connections that make use of the categorical nature of the structures involved. As already mentioned, differential bundles and linear morphisms form a tangent category denoted by
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, whose tangent structure obtained by lifting the one of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. We would like to show that Koszul connections are precisely Erhesmann connections in this category. To this end, we begin with a few technical results.

We have mentioned in Example
2.8
, that every object
M
of a tangent category admits a trivial differential bundle, denoted by
\mathbf{0}_{M}
. If
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
is a differential bundle in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, it is easy to see that the morphism
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)\colon\mathbf{q}\to\mathbf{0}_{M}
becomes a linear morphism of differential bundles. We shall now prove that such a linear morphism admits sufficient pullbacks in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
provided
q
is a tangent display map in the base tangent category. Unfortunately, in general,
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)
fails to become a full tangent display map in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. However, the existing pullbacks suffice for our goals.

Lemma 3.56
.

For a given tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, the linear morphism
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)\colon\mathbf{q}\to\mathbf{0}_{M}
of differential bundles admits all tangent pullbacks along maps of type
(f,f)\colon\mathbf{0}_{M^{\prime}}\to\mathbf{0}_{M}
in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
provided that
q
these tangent pullbacks exist in the base tangent category.

Proof.

Consider a map of differential bundles
(f,f)\colon\mathbf{0}_{M^{\prime}}\to\mathbf{0}_{M}
, where
f\colon M^{\prime}\to M
is a morphism of the base tangent category. Thanks to
[
4
, Lemma 2.7]
, we already know that differential bundles are stable under tangent pullbacks. Therefore, we may define
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
as the differential bundle in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
obtained by pulling back
\mathbf{q}
along
f
, as in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

[
4
, Lemma 2.7]
also tells us that the morphism
(f,g)\colon\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{q}
becomes a linear morphism of differential bundles and thus a map in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. We may use this fact to draw a diagram in the category of differential bundles, as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{M^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id}_{M^{\prime}},q^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,f)}

Our goal is to prove that this diagram is in fact a tangent pullback. To this end, we start by considering two morphisms
(\alpha_{\downarrow},\alpha^{\uparrow})\colon\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbf{0}_{M^{\prime}}
and
(\beta_{\downarrow},\beta^{\uparrow})\colon\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
of differential bundles, making the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{M^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta_{\downarrow},\beta^{\uparrow})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{\downarrow},\alpha^{\uparrow})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id}_{M^{\prime}},q^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,f)}

commutative. Using that
\mathbf{0}_{M^{\prime}}
is trivial, we have that
\alpha^{\uparrow}=q^{\prime\prime}\alpha_{\downarrow}
. Using the universal property of the tangent pullback that defines
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
, we may also define a morphism
\gamma\colon E^{\prime\prime}\to E^{\prime}
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\uparrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\uparrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\uparrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

However, since
\alpha^{\uparrow}=q^{\prime\prime}\alpha_{\downarrow}
, we compute that
\gamma^{\uparrow}q^{\prime}=\alpha^{\uparrow}=q^{\prime\prime}\alpha_{\downarrow}
. Moreover, using that the pullback that defines
q^{\prime}
is also a tangent pullback, we can prove that
(\gamma_{\downarrow},\gamma^{\uparrow})\colon\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
is linear, where
\gamma^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\alpha_{\downarrow}
. Using the linearity of
(\alpha_{\downarrow},\alpha^{\uparrow})
and
(\beta_{\downarrow},\beta^{\uparrow})
, it is not hard to convince ourselves that the following two equations

\displaystyle l_{q^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{T}\gamma^{\uparrow}\mathrm{T}g=\beta^{\uparrow}l_{q}

\displaystyle l_{q^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{T}\gamma^{\uparrow}\mathrm{T}q^{\prime}=\alpha^{\uparrow}z_{M^{\prime}}

hold. However, also
(f,g)
and
(\mathsf{id}_{M^{\prime}},q^{\prime})
are linear, and therefore, for a similar argument, also the following two equations must hold:

\displaystyle\gamma^{\uparrow}l_{q^{\prime}}\mathrm{T}g=\beta^{\uparrow}l_{q}

\displaystyle\gamma^{\uparrow}l_{q^{\prime}}\mathrm{T}q^{\prime}=\alpha^{\uparrow}z_{M^{\prime}}

Therefore, we must conclude that
(\gamma_{\downarrow},\gamma^{\uparrow})
is a linear morphism. Furthermore,
(\gamma_{\downarrow},\gamma^{\uparrow})
makes the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{M^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{\downarrow},\gamma^{\uparrow})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta_{\downarrow},\beta^{\uparrow})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{\downarrow},\alpha^{\uparrow})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id}_{M^{\prime}},q^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,f)}

commutative and, by the universal property of the pullback that defines
q^{\prime}
it must also be the unique such map. This proves that
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)
admits all the pullbacks along the maps of type
(f,f)\colon\mathbf{0}_{M^{\prime}}\to\mathbf{0}_{M}
. To prove that these pullbacks are in fact tangent pullbacks, we can apply the same argument to
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}^{n}M},\mathrm{T}^{n}q)
and to
(\mathrm{T}^{n}f,\mathrm{T}^{n}f)\colon\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm{T}^{n}M^{\prime}}\to\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm{T}^{n}M}
.
∎

Despite
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)\colon\mathbf{q}\to\mathbf{0}_{M}
not fullfilling the requirements of being a tangent display map, it still admits enough pullbacks to admit both the constructions of the vertical and the Finsler bundles. In particular, it admits the vertical bundle and the Finsler bundle, which are the differential bundles

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)^{\mathrm{V}}=(\mathsf{id}_{E},\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}})\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q\to\mathbf{0}_{E}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)^{\mathrm{F}}=(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}})\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\to\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm{T}M}

respectively. Therefore, it makes sense to talk about Ehresmann connections on
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)
. We can now give our characterization.

Theorem 3.57
.

Consider a display differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. The following are equivalent.

[1]

(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
is a linear Ehresmann connection on
\mathbf{q}
;

[2]

(\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{R}},\mathrm{H})
is a Koszul connection on
\mathbf{q}
, where
\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{R}}
is defined as in Proposition
3.54
;

[3]

((p_{M},\mathrm{R}),(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\mathrm{H}))
is an Ehresmann connection on
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)
in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Furthermore, if
((\mathrm{R}_{\downarrow},\mathrm{R}^{\uparrow}),(\mathrm{H}_{\downarrow},\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow}))
is an Ehresmann connection on
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)
in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, then
\mathrm{R}_{\downarrow}
and
\mathrm{H}_{\downarrow}
are necessarily
p_{M}
and
\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M}
, respectively.

Proof.

We have already proved the equivalence between
[1]
and
[2]
in Theorem
3.55
. It is only left to prove the equivalence between
[2]
and
[3]
. Consider a linear Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
on
\mathbf{q}
. For starters, notice that, by virtue of the axioms
[VLC]
and
[HLC]
,
(p_{M},\mathrm{R})\colon\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q}\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}\mathbf{q}
and
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\mathrm{H})\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}\mathbf{q}\to\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q}
become morphisms in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Furthermore, thanks to Lemma
3.56
, the vertical and the Finsler bundle of
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)
are constructed directly using the pullbacks defining the vertical and the Finsler bundle of
\mathbf{q}
. Therefore, all the equational axioms of an Ehresmann connection on
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)
follow directly from the ones satisfied by
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
. Therefore,
((p_{M},\mathrm{R}),(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\mathrm{H}))
becomes in fact an Ehresmann connection on
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)
. Now, consider an Ehresmann connection
((\mathrm{R}_{\downarrow},\mathrm{R}^{\uparrow}),(\mathrm{H}_{\downarrow},\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow}))
of
(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)
in
\mathsf{DB}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. However, since by
[VC.1]
,
(\mathrm{R}_{\downarrow},\mathrm{R}^{\uparrow})\colon p_{\mathbf{q}}^{\mathsf{DB}}\to(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)^{\mathrm{V}}
is required to be a retraction of the inclusion
\iota_{(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)}=(z_{M},\iota_{q})
,
z_{M}\mathrm{R}_{\downarrow}=\mathsf{id}_{M}=z_{M}p_{M}
, which implies that
\mathrm{R}_{\downarrow}=p_{M}
, since
z_{M}
is monic. Furthermore, since by
[HC.1]

(\mathrm{H}_{\downarrow},\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow})\colon(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)^{\mathrm{F}}\to p_{\mathbf{q}}^{\mathsf{DB}}
is required to be a section of the horizontal descent
\pi_{(\mathsf{id}_{M},q)}=(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\pi_{q})
, it follows that
\mathrm{H}_{\downarrow}=\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M}
.
[VLC]
and
[HLC]
follow from
(p_{M},\mathrm{R}^{\uparrow})
and
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow})
being linear morphisms, while all the other equational axioms follow from the other axioms satisfied by
((p_{M},\mathrm{R}^{\uparrow}),(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\mathrm{H}^{\uparrow}))
.
∎

4
Vertical, Finsler, and horizontal vector fields

In differential geometry, a
vertical
vector field of a submersion
q\colon E\to M
consists of a vector field
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
of
E
such that, for each
y\in E
, the corresponding tangent vector
X_{y}\in\mathrm{T}_{y}E
is vertical, that is, it belongs to the kernel of
\mathsf{d}_{y}q\colon\mathrm{T}_{y}E\to\mathrm{T}_{q(y)}M
. The vertical tangent vectors of
q
form the vertical bundle of
q
, that we studied in Section
2.2
.

When an Ehresmann connection is provided, there is a prescribed way to
split
the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}E
of the total space
E
into vertical tangent vectors and
horizontal
tangent vectors, which are those tangent vectors in the image of the horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}E
or, equivalently, in the kernel of the vertical connection
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to\mathrm{V}q
.

While the vertical tangent vectors capture the degrees of freedom of the total space
E
that do not change the base point of
M
, the
horizontal
tangent vectors capture those degrees of freedom of
E
that are
parallel
to
M
, that is, that change the base point
M
without changing the vertical degree of freedom of
E
. A horizontal vector field of
q
consists of a vector field
Y\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
of
E
such that, for each
y\in E
, the corresponding tangent vector
Y_{y}\in\mathrm{T}_{y}E
is horizontal.

In this section, we introduce vertical and horizontal vector fields in tangent categories. These concepts will be important later when we discuss the curvature and parallel transport of an Ehresmann connection.

4.1
Vertical vector fields

A vector field on an object
M
in a tangent category is a section of the tangent bundle
[
19
,
1
]
, that is, a morphism
X\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
subject to the equation
Xp_{M}=\mathsf{id}_{M}
. As proved in
[
1
,
2
]
, in a tangent category with negatives, there is a well-defined notion of Lie bracket that generalize the usual notion of differential geometry. We briefly recall this construction, since it will be important later.

As previously discussed for differential bundles, given a morphism
f\colon X\to\mathrm{T}^{2}M
satisfying the equation
f\mathrm{T}p_{M}=fp_{\mathrm{T}M}p_{M}z_{M}
, by the universal property of the vertical lift, there exists a unique morphism
\tilde{f}\colon N\to\mathrm{T}_{2}M
satisfying the following equation.
\tilde{f}\xi_{M}=f
, where
\xi_{M}=(l_{M}\times_{z_{M}}z_{\mathrm{T}M})\mathrm{T}s_{M}
. We recommend the reader check
[
1
]
for details. Since
\mathrm{T}_{2}M
is the pullback of
p_{M}
along itself, it comes with two projections,
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}M\to\mathrm{T}M
. The morphism
\tilde{f}\pi_{1}
is denoted by
\{f\}\colon N\to\mathrm{T}M
.

Now, consider two maps
f,g\colon N\to\mathrm{T}M
of
M
. If
fp_{M}=gp_{M}
, we can sum
f
and
g
together as follows:

\displaystyle f+g\colon N\xrightarrow{\langle X,Y\rangle}\mathrm{T}_{2}M\xrightarrow{s_{M}}\mathrm{T}M

Using this notation, we may denote by
0
the vector field
z_{M}
and also by
-f
, the morphism
fn_{M}
, where
n_{M}
denotes the negation
n_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}M
.

Now, consider two vector fields
X,Y\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
of
M
. The Lie bracket of
X
and
Y
is the vector field
[X,Y]
, defined by the formula:

\displaystyle[X,Y]\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{X\mathrm{T}Y-Y\mathrm{T}Xc_{M}\right\}

We shall now introduce the notion of vertical vector fields, which are those vector fields which pick out vertical tangent vectors of
q
.

Definition 4.1
.

A
vertical vector field
on a map
q\colon E\to M
is of a vector field
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
on
E
in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

Similarly to ordinary vector fields
[
5
, Proposition 2.10]
, vertical vector fields form a tangent category.

Proposition 4.2
.

There is a tangent category
\mathsf{VVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, whose objects are pairs
(q,X)
formed by a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
and a vertical vector field
X
on
q
. A morphism of
\mathsf{VVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
from
(q,X)
to
(q^{\prime},X^{\prime})
is a morphism
(f,g)\colon q\to q^{\prime}
of tangent display maps, that is, a pair of morphisms
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
and
g\colon E\to E^{\prime}
that commute with
q
and
q^{\prime}
. Furthermore, a morphism
(f,g)
is required to make the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}

The tangent bundle functor
\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{VVF}}
of
\mathsf{VVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
sends a pair
(q,X)
to
(\mathrm{T}q,X_{\mathrm{T}})
, where we define:

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{T}}\colon\mathrm{T}E\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}X}\mathrm{T}^{2}E\xrightarrow{c_{E}}\mathrm{T}^{2}E

Finally, the structural natural transformations of
\mathsf{VVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
are the same as the base category.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
[
5
, Proposition 2.10]
.
∎

As already mentioned, for a given tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and an object
M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, one can define a new tangent category, denoted
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M
and called the slice tangent category on
M
. The objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M
are tangent display maps
q\colon E\to M
with
M
per codomain, and morphisms
f\colon q\to q^{\prime}
are morphisms of
\mathbb{X}
that commute with
q
and
q^{\prime}
, that is,
fq^{\prime}=q
. The tangent bundle functor
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}
sends an object
q\colon E\to M
to
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q\colon\mathrm{V}q\to M
, that is, the pullback of
\mathrm{T}q
along the zero
z_{M}
of Equation (
2.1
), and a morphism
f\colon q\to q^{\prime}
to the unique morphism
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}f\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q^{\prime}
satisfying
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}f\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q^{\prime}=\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q
and
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}f\iota_{q^{\prime}}=\iota_{q}\mathrm{T}f
. The projection
p^{\mathrm{V}}_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q\to q
is given by
\iota_{q}p_{E}
, while the other structural natural transformations are constructed using the universal property of tangent pullbacks. We suggest the reader to see
[
7
, Section 3.3]
for details.

Our next goal is to show that one may view vertical vector fields as vector fields in the slice tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M
. To prove this, we will first establish several technical results. We say that a functor
F\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
between two tangent categories
creates tangent limits
when, given a diagram
D\colon I\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, if the diagram
F\circ D\colon I\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
admits a tangent limit cone
\varphi_{c}\colon M\to FD(c)
in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, there exists a cone
\psi_{c}\colon L\to D(c)
of
D
on
\mathbb{X}
such that
FL=M
,
F\psi_{c}=\varphi_{c}
, and
\psi_{c}
is a tangent limit cone for
D
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}\colon\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
creates tangent limits.

Proof.

First, we show that
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}
is conservative, that is,
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}
reflects isomorphisms. To this end, consider a morphism
f\colon(M,X)\to(M^{\prime},X^{\prime})
of vector fields which is an isomorphism in the base tangent category, with inverse
g\colon M^{\prime}\to M
. We want to show that
g
is a morphism of vector fields
g\colon(M^{\prime},X^{\prime})\to(M,X)
:

\displaystyle gX

\displaystyle=\penalty\ gX\mathrm{T}f\mathrm{T}g

(
fg=\mathsf{id}_{M}
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ gfX^{\prime}\mathrm{T}g

(
X\mathrm{T}f=fX^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ X^{\prime}\mathrm{T}g

(
gf=\mathsf{id}_{M^{\prime}}
)

The next step is to show that for each diagram
D\colon J\to\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, if
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}\circ D
admits a tangent limit cone, there exists a tangent limit cone of
D
preserved by
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}
. Consider such a diagram together with a tangent limit cone
\varphi_{c}\colon L\to\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c))
of
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}\circ D
in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. In particular,
\mathrm{T}\varphi_{c}\colon\mathrm{T}L\to\mathrm{T}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c)))
is a limit cone of
\mathrm{T}\circ\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}\circ D
. Let
D(c)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(M_{c},X_{c})
be the vector field corresponding to
c\in I
. Define
Y\colon L\to\mathrm{T}L
as the unique morphism making the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{c}}

commutative, where
X_{c}\colon\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c))\to\mathrm{T}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c)))
since
X_{c}
is a vector field. However, by the universality of the limit cone,
Yp
is the unique morphism making the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{D(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{c}}

commute. Thus,
Yp
must be the identity, since
X_{c}p_{D(c)}=\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c))}
, namely,
Y
is a vector field on
L
. Furthermore, each
\varphi_{c}
becomes a morphism of vector fields
\varphi_{c}\colon(L,Y)\to D(c)
. Let us prove that the cone
\varphi_{c}\colon(L,Y)\to D(c)
is a limit cone in
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
for
D
. To this end, consider another cone
\psi_{c}\colon(M,X)\to D(c)=(M_{c},X_{c})
. Since
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}
preserves the cone
\varphi_{c}
, we obtain a unique morphism
\xi\colon M\to L
in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{c}}

Let us prove that
\xi
is a morphism of vector fields. Notice that
\xi Y\colon M\to\mathrm{T}L
is the unique morphism making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{c}}

However, so does
X\mathrm{T}\xi
, since:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}(D(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\varphi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\psi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{c}}

Therefore, by the universality of
L
in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
,
\xi Y
must coincide with
X\mathrm{T}\xi
, namely,
\xi
is a morphism of vector fields. Finally, since
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}
is conservative and preserves the limit cone
\varphi_{c}\colon(L,Y)\to D(c)
,
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}
creates tangent limits
[
9
]
.
∎

The next proposition shows that Cartesian tangent morphisms which create tangent limits
reflect tangent display maps
, that is, if
q\colon E\to M
is a map of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
such that
Fq
is tangent display in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, then
q
is a tangent display map in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Cartesian tangent morphisms that create tangent limits reflect tangent display maps.

Proof.

Consider a strong tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and a map
q\colon E\to M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
such that
Fq
is tangent display in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. For starters, we realize that, for each
n\geq 0
, since
(F,\alpha)
is Cartesian, the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\mathrm{T}^{n}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}F\mathrm{T}^{n-1}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}FE}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\mathrm{T}^{n}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}F\mathrm{T}^{n-1}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}FM}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathrm{T}^{n-1}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\mathrm{T}^{n}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\alpha_{\mathrm{T}^{n-2}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}F\mathrm{T}^{n-1}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n-1}\alpha_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n}Fq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\mathrm{T}^{n-1}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\alpha_{\mathrm{T}^{n-2}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}^{n-1}\alpha_{M}}

is a tangent pullback. Since tangent display maps are stable under tangent pullbacks, this implies that
F\mathrm{T}^{n}q
is also tangent display. Now, consider a morphism
f\colon N\to\mathrm{T}^{n}M
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Since
F\mathrm{T}^{n}q
is tangent display, the tangent pullback of
Ff
along
F\mathrm{T}^{n}q
exists in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. However, since
F
creates tangent limits, the tangent pullback of
f
along
\mathrm{T}^{n}q
exists in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Therefore,
q
is a tangent display map in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.
∎

Unfortunately, in general, a tangent display map of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
fails to be a tangent display map of the base tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. However, thanks to Proposition
4.4
, we now show that, if
q
is a morphism of vector fields which is a tangent display map in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, then
q
is also a tangent display map in
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Corollary 4.5
.

The forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}\colon\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
reflects tangent display maps.

Proof.

This is a direct application of Lemma
4.3
and Proposition
4.4
.
∎

We can now prove that vertical vector fields can be regarded as vector fields in the slice category (and are also equivalent to two other formulations).

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
be a morphism in a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
q\colon E\to M
a tangent display map. The following are equivalent:

[1]

X
is a vertical vector field on
q
;

[2]

X
factors through
\iota_{q}
by defining a vector field
\hat{X}\colon q\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q
in the slice tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M
over
q
;

[3]

X
is a vector field and
q\colon(E,X)\to(M,z_{M})
is a morphism of vector fields;

[4]

q\colon(E,X)\to(M,z_{M})
is an object of the slice tangent category
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/(M,z_{M})
of the tangent category of vector fields
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

For starters, let us show that [1] implies [2]. Consider a vertical vector field
X
of a tangent display map
q^{E}_{M}
. Thus,
q
is an object of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M
. Moreover, since
X\mathrm{T}q=qz_{M}
,
X
factors through
\iota_{q}
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

Moreover, since
X
is a vector field:

\displaystyle\hat{X}p^{\mathrm{V}}_{q}=\hat{X}\iota_{q}p_{E}=Xp_{E}=\mathsf{id}_{E}

Thus,
\hat{X}
becomes a vector field on
q
in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M
.

Next, consider a vector field
\hat{X}\colon q\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q
on
q
in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M
; let
X
be
\hat{X}\iota_{q}
.
X
is clearly a vector field of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, since
Xp_{E}=\hat{X}\iota_{q}p_{E}=\hat{X}p^{\mathrm{V}}_{q}=\mathsf{id}_{E}
.
Moreover, by assumption,
\hat{X}
is a morphism in the slice tangent category, thus,
\hat{X}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q=q
. We shall compute:

\displaystyle X\mathrm{T}q=\hat{X}\iota_{q}\mathrm{T}q=\hat{X}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}qz_{M}=qz_{M}

Therefore,
q\colon(E,X)\to(M,z_{M})
becomes a morphism of vector fields. To prove that
q
defines an object in the slice tangent category
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/(M,z_{M})
, we only need to prove that
q
is in fact a tangent display map in
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. However, by Corollary
4.5
, the forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}^{\mathsf{VF}}\colon\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
reflects tangent display maps. Thus, since
q
is tangent display in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, it becomes a tangent display map in
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Finally, let us assume [4]; namely that
q\colon(E,X)\to(M,z_{M})
is an object of
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/(M,z_{M})
. In particular,
X
is a vector field on
E
in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Moreover, since
q
is a morphism of vector fields,
X\mathrm{T}q=qz_{M}
, thus,
X
is a vector field on
q
in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.
∎

Using the characterization provided by Theorem
4.6
, we shall now prove that, given a vertical connection, vertical vector fields are those vector fields
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
satisfying the equation
X=X\phi
.

Corollary 4.7
.

Given a vertical connection on
q\colon E\to M
, a vector field
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
of
E
is vertical if and only if
X=X\phi
, where
\phi
is the vertical connection form.

Proof.

Suppose that a vector field
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
satisfies
X=X\phi
. By definition,
\phi=\mathrm{R}\iota_{q}
, where
\mathrm{R}
is the vertical connection. Thus,
X=X\mathrm{R}\iota_{q}
. However,
X\mathrm{R}q^{\mathrm{V}}=Xp_{E}=\mathsf{id}_{E}
, since
X
is a vector field and
\mathrm{R}\colon\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\to\mathbf{p}_{E}
is a bundle morphism. Thus,
X\mathrm{R}q^{\mathrm{V}}
is a section of the vertical bundle and therefore,
X
is necessarily vertical. Now, suppose that
X
is a vertical vector field. Thus,
X=\hat{X}\iota_{q}
for some section
\hat{X}
of the vertical bundle. Thus,
X\phi=\hat{X}\iota_{q}\phi
. However, by
[VCF.1]
,
\iota_{q}\phi=\iota_{q}
. Thus,
X\phi=\hat{X}\iota_{q}=X
.
∎

4.2
Finsler and horizontal vector fields

In this section, we introduce Finsler and horizontal vector fields.

Definition 4.8
.

A
Finsler vector field
on a map
q\colon E\to M
consists of a morphism
A\colon E\to\mathrm{T}M
subject to the following condition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

Remark 4.9
.

It is important to realize that a Finsler vector field does not need to be a vector field in any meaningful way. This naming convention will be clear later.

Just like vertical vector fields, Finsler vector fields form a tangent category.

Proposition 4.10
.

There is a tangent category
\mathsf{FVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
with the following structure. An object of
\mathsf{FVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
consists of a pair
(q,A)
formed by a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
and a Finsler vector field
A
on
q
; a morphism
(f,g)\colon(q,A)\to(q^{\prime},A^{\prime})
of
\mathsf{FVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
consists of a pair of morphisms
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
and
g\colon E\to E^{\prime}
that commute with
q
and
q^{\prime}
, that is,
qf=gq^{\prime}
, and commute with the Finsler vector fields, that is,
A\mathrm{T}f=A^{\prime}g
. The tangent bundle functor
\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{FVF}}\colon\mathsf{FVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{FVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
sends a pair
(q,A)
to
(\mathrm{T}q,A_{\mathrm{T}})
, where we define:

\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{T}}\colon\mathrm{T}E\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}A}\mathrm{T}^{2}M\xrightarrow{c}\mathrm{T}^{2}M

Finally, the structural natural transformations of
\mathsf{FVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
are the same as the base category.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
[
5
, Proposition 2.10]
.
∎

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
A\colon E\to\mathrm{T}M
be a morphism in a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and let
q\colon E\to M
be a tangent display map. The following are equivalent:

[1]

A
is a Finsler vector field on
q
;

[2]

A
factors through
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}M
by defining a section
A_{\mathrm{F}}\colon E\to\mathrm{F}q
of the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
.

Proof.

For starters, suppose that
A
is a Finsler vector field on
q
. Thus, by using the universal property of the Finsler bundle, we can define a unique morphism
A_{\mathrm{F}}\colon E\to\mathrm{F}q
making the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

By construction,
A_{\mathrm{F}}
is a section of the Finsler bundle. Now, assume that
A
factors through
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q
via a section
A_{\mathrm{F}}\colon E\to\mathrm{F}q
of the Finsler bundle. Thus, we shall compute:

\displaystyle Ap_{M}=A_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}qp_{M}=A_{\mathrm{F}}q^{\mathrm{F}}q=q

Therefore,
A
is a Finsler vector field.
∎

Thanks to the characterization of Theorem
4.11
, in the presence of a horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}
, a Finsler vector field
A
can be used to define a vector field over
E
obtained by composing the corresponding section
A_{\mathrm{F}}
of the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
by
\mathrm{H}
, that is,
A_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{H}
. Those vector fields of that form are known as
horizontal
vector fields.

Definition 4.12
.

Given a horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}E
on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
, a
\mathrm{H}
-horizontal vector field
on
q
consists of a vector field
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
of
E
such that, there exists a (necessarily unique) Finsler vector field
A\colon E\to\mathrm{T}M
satisfying
X=A_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{H}
, where
A_{\mathrm{F}}
is the associated section of the Finsler bundle of
q
.

In Corollary
4.7
, we showed that a vector field
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
is vertical if and only if
X
satisfies the equation
X=X\phi
, provided the existence of a vertical connection. Similarly, horizontal vector fields are those vector fields that satisfy
X=X\psi
, where
\psi
denotes the horizontal connection form.

Corollary 4.13
.

Given a horizontal connection on
q\colon E\to M
, a vector field
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
of
E
is horizontal if and only if
X=X\psi
, where
\psi
is the horizontal connection form.

Proof.

Suppose that a vector field
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
satisfies
X=X\psi
. By definition,
\psi=\pi_{q}\mathrm{H}
, where
\mathrm{H}
is the horizontal connection. Thus,
X=X\pi_{q}\mathrm{H}
. However,
X\pi_{q}q^{\mathrm{F}}=Xp_{E}=\mathsf{id}_{E}
, where we used that
X
is a vector field and that
\pi_{q}q^{\mathrm{F}}=p_{E}
. Thus,
X\pi_{q}
is a section of the Finsler bundle and therefore,
X
is necessarily horizontal. Now, suppose that
X
is a horizontal vector field. Thus,
X=A_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{H}
for some section
A_{\mathrm{F}}
of the Finsler bundle. Thus,
X\psi=A_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{H}
. However, by
[HCF.1]
,
\mathrm{H}\psi=\mathrm{H}
. Thus,
X\psi=A_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{H}\psi=A_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{H}=X
.
∎

4.3
The decomposition induced by the connection

Thanks to Theorem
3.21
, an Ehresmann connection on a submersion is equivalent to a splitting of the fundamental short exact sequence. A splitting allows one to decompose the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{E}
as a biproduct
\mathbf{p}_{E}\cong\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\oplus_{E}\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
, that is,
\mathrm{T}E\cong\mathrm{V}q\oplus_{E}\mathrm{F}q
, where
\oplus_{E}
is a biproduct in the category of differential bundles over
E
(Corollary
3.23
). This decomposition induces a decomposition of vector fields into a vertical and a horizontal component.

Theorem 4.14
.

Given an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
on a tangent display map, every vector field
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
can be uniquely decomposed into a vertical and a horizontal component:

\displaystyle X=X_{\mathrm{V}}+X_{\mathrm{H}}

where
X_{\mathrm{V}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=X\phi
is a vertical vector field and
X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=X\psi
is a horizontal vector field.

Proof.

For starters, let us show that
X_{\mathrm{V}}+X_{\mathrm{H}}
correspond to
X
. Using
[ECF.2]
, we shall compute:

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{V}}+X_{\mathrm{H}}=X\phi+X\psi=X(\phi+\psi)=X\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}=X

Now, suppose that
X^{\prime}_{\mathrm{V}}
and
X^{\prime}_{\mathrm{H}}
are vertical and horizontal vector fields such that
X=X^{\prime}_{\mathrm{V}}+X^{\prime}_{\mathrm{H}}
. By Corollaries
4.7
and
4.13
,
X^{\prime}_{\mathrm{V}}=X^{\prime}_{\mathrm{V}}\phi
and
X^{\prime}_{\mathrm{H}}=X^{\prime}_{\mathrm{H}}\psi
. Therefore, by using
[ECF.1]
, we can prove:

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{V}}=X\phi=(X_{\mathrm{V}}^{\prime}+X_{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime})\phi=X_{\mathrm{V}}^{\prime}\phi\phi+X_{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime}\psi\phi=X_{\mathrm{V}}^{\prime}\phi+X_{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime}p_{E}z_{E}=X_{\mathrm{V}}^{\prime}+0=X_{\mathrm{V}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{H}}=X\psi=(X_{\mathrm{V}}^{\prime}+X_{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime})\psi=X_{\mathrm{V}}^{\prime}\phi\psi+X_{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime}\psi\psi=X_{\mathrm{V}}^{\prime}p_{E}z_{E}+X_{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime}\psi=0+X_{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime}=X_{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime}

This proves that the decomposition is necessarily unique.
∎

4.4
The Finsler tangent category

Theorem
4.6
characterizes vertical vector fields as vector fields in the slice tangent category. In this section, we take a brief detour from the paper’s main story to explore the following question. Can Finsler vector fields be characterized as vector fields in a suitable tangent category? This exploration, which we believe is worth including, will not, however, be necessary to the rest of the paper. Therefore, the reader may feel free to skip to the next section.

We shall start by observing that the tangent structure of the slice tangent category is induced, in some appropriate sense, by the vertical bundle. In fact, in the slice, the tangent bundle of an object
q
,
q
being a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
, is the object
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q
, which corresponds to the morphism
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q\colon\mathrm{V}q\to M
, obtained by pulling back
\mathrm{T}q
along
z_{M}
, as in Diagram (
2.1
). Moreover, the underlying map of the projection
p^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q\to q
corresponds to the projection
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}=\iota_{q}p_{E}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
of the vertical bundle of
q
. Inspired by this observation, we shall construct a new tangent category, denoted by
\mathrm{F}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
as follows.

The underlying category is the category
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
tangent display maps.

The tangent bundle functor
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}\colon\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
sends each
q\colon E\to M
to
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\colon\mathrm{F}E\to\mathrm{T}M
, defined by the tangent pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

Furthermore,
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}
sends a morphism
(f,g)\colon q\to q^{\prime}
to the morphism
(\mathrm{T}f,\mathrm{F}_{f}g)\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q^{\prime}
defined by Lemma
2.26
. Next, we shall describe the structural natural transformations of the tangent structure
\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}}
, starting from the projection. The projection
p^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\to q
corresponds to the pair
p^{\mathrm{F}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(p_{M},q^{\mathrm{F}})
. Now, recall that, since
q^{\mathrm{F}}
is a tangent pullback of a differential bundle,
\mathbf{p}_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to M
being the tangent bundle, also
q^{\mathrm{F}}
acquires the structure of a differential bundle, which is, by definition, the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
of
q
. Using the zero
z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
and the sum
s_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
morphisms of
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
, we may introduce the zero and sum morphism of
(\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}})
as the following commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}_{2}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\times_{M}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{M}}

respectively. Finally, we introduce the vertical lift and the canonical flip, by invoking once again the universal property of the tangent pullback that defines
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q)^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q)^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q)^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

Lemma 4.15
.

The following diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}_{2}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\times_{M}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{M}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{M}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{M}}

are tangent pullbacks in the base tangent category.

Proof.

The first diagram is simply the tangent pullback diagram of Equation (
6.1
). Now, consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

The right square diagram is the first diagram, which we have already shown to be a tangent pullback. Moreover, the outer diagram is straightforwardly a tangent pullback since
z_{M}p_{M}=\mathsf{id}_{M}
and
z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}q^{\mathrm{F}}=\mathsf{id}_{E}
. Therefore, by Lemma
2.6
, the left square diagram is also a tangent pullback. To prove that the third diagram is a tangent pullback, we shall adopt a similar technique. Let us start by considering the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}_{2}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q\times_{M}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}

(4.1)

Once yet, the right diagram is a tangent pullback. Moreover,
s_{M}p_{M}=\pi_{1}p_{M}=\pi_{2}p_{M}
and
z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}q^{\mathrm{F}}=\pi_{1}q^{\mathrm{F}}=\pi_{2}q^{\mathrm{F}}
. We shall prove that the outer diagram is again a tangent pullback, by considering the following general situation. Consider two tangent display maps
q\colon E\to M
and

and a morphism
(f,g)\colon q\to q^{\prime}
such that the commutative square
fq=gq^{\prime}
is a tangent pullback. Now, consider two maps
\alpha\colon X\to M^{\prime}
and
\beta\colon X\to E
making the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
