\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times_{f}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}

commutative. As represented in the diagram, from the universal property of the tangent pullback given by
(f,g)\colon q\to q^{\prime}
, there exists a unique map
\gamma\colon X\to E
, and by using the universal property of the top face diagram, we construct a unique map
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\colon X\to E_{2}
, proving that the desired diagram, is in fact a tangent pullback. In particular, the outer diagram of Equation (
4.1
) is then a tangent pullback and therefore, by invoking once again Lemma
2.6
, the third diagram is a tangent pullback. Finally, to prove that the last two diagrams are also tangent pullbacks, we may consider the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{T}^{F}q)^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathrm{T}M}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q)^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathrm{T}M}}

Once again, in both scenarios, the right hand square diagram is a tangent pullback. Furthermore, using that
l_{M}p_{\mathrm{T}M}=p_{M}z_{M}
and that
l_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}(\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q)^{\mathrm{F}}=q^{\mathrm{F}}z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
, and that
c_{M}p_{\mathrm{T}M}=\mathrm{T}p_{M}
and
c_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}(\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q)^{\mathrm{F}}=\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}(q^{\mathrm{F}})
, we shall rewrite the outer diagrams as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}p_{M}}

The left hand side diagram is two tangent pullback diagrams stack to each other, while, for the right hand side one, we can argue it is a tangent pullback from the preservation of the pullback by the tangent bundle functor.
∎

Proposition 4.16
.

There is a tangent structure
\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}}
over the category
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of tangent display maps of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, whose tangent bundle functor and structural natural transformations are
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}
,
p^{\mathrm{F}}
,
z^{\mathrm{F}}
,
s^{\mathrm{F}}
,
l^{\mathrm{F}}
, and
c^{\mathrm{F}}
. Furthermore, if
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
admits negatives, so does
(\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}})
. Finally, the horizontal descent
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\pi_{q})\colon\mathrm{T}q\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q
induces a morphism of tangent structures
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\pi_{q})\colon\mathbb{T}\Rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}}
on
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

We shall not complete all the details of this proof, since it is rather technical and not particularly enlightning. Instead, we simply argue that, by Lemma
4.15
, all the diagrams that define the structural natural transformations of
\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}}
are in fact tangent pullbacks in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, the equational axioms of a tangent category follow directly from
\mathbb{T}
being a tangent structure and by the universal property of these tangent pullbacks. To establish the universal property of the vertical lift, consider a morphism
(f,g)\colon q^{\prime}\to{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q
which equalizes
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}p^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}
and
p^{\mathrm{F}}_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}p^{\mathrm{F}}qz^{\mathrm{F}}_{q}
. Using the universal property of the vertical lift
l_{M}
, we obtain a unique morphism
\hat{f}\colon M^{\prime}\to\mathrm{T}_{2}M
. Moreover, from the universal properties of the tangent pullbacks of Lemma
4.15
, we can argue that the map
(\xi_{M},\xi_{q}^{\mathrm{F}})\colon\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}_{2}q\to{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q
that establishes the universal property of the lift, defines a tangent pullback in the base category, as well. Therefore, we can pull back
g
along
\xi_{M}
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{2}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}_{2}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}}^{2}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

This proves the universal property of the vertica lift for
(\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}})
. We leave it to the reader to show that the horizontal descent defines a morphism of tangent structures.
∎

One may argue that, since the commutative square diagrams that correspond to the definition of the structural natural transformations of the tangent category
(\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}})
are tangent pullbacks by Lemma
4.15
, one may restrict the whole tangent category to a subcategory of
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
defined as follows. We denote by
\mathscr{D}_{\mathsf{pb}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
the subcategory of
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
with the same objects of
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
but where morpshisms are pairs
(f,g)\colon q\to q^{\prime}
for which the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a tangent pullback in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proposition 4.17
.

The tangent structure
\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}}
of Proposition
4.16
restricts to the subcategory
\mathscr{D}_{\mathsf{pb}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, defining a new tangent category
(\mathscr{D}_{\mathsf{pb}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}})
.

Proof.

We leave the proof of this proposition to the reader.
∎

Definition 4.18
.

We call the tangent category of Proposition
4.16
the
Finsler tangent category
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, while the tangent category of Proposition
4.17
will be called the
strong Finsler tangent category
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. We shall denote the former by
\mathrm{F}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, and the latter by
\mathrm{F}_{\mathsf{pb}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Our goal was to construct a tangent category whose vector fields correspond to Finsler vector fields. Our constructions of the strong and the non-strong Finsler tangent categories get very close to this.

Lemma 4.19
.

A vector field in either the Finsler tangent category
\mathrm{F}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
or the strong Finsler tangent category
\mathrm{F}_{\mathsf{pb}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
consists precisely of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
together with a pair
(X,A_{\mathrm{F}})
formed by an ordinary vector field
X\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
on
M
in the base tangent category together with a section
A_{\mathrm{F}}\colon E\to\mathrm{F}q
of the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
of
q
, satisfying the following condition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{F}q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

Furthermore, this diagram is necessarily a tangent pullback.

By Theorem
4.11
, sections of the Finsler bundle are in bijective correspondence with Finsler vector fields. Unfortunately, not every Finsler vector field
A\colon E\to\mathrm{T}M
has an underlying ordinary vector field
X\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
.

Definition 4.20
.

A
full Finsler vector field
over a map
q\colon E\to M
consists of a pair
(X,A)
formed by a vector field
X\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
on
M
together with a Finsler vector field
A\colon E\to\mathrm{T}M
over
q
, satisfying the following condition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

Theorem 4.21
.

Consider a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
. Given a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
, the following are equivalent:

[1]

A full vector field
(X,A)
over
q
;

[2]

A vector field
(X,A_{\mathrm{F}})
over
q
in the Finsler tangent category;

[3]

A vector field
(X,A_{\mathrm{F}})
over
q
in the strong Finsler tangent category.

In particular, full Finsler vector fields over tangent display maps define a tangent category
\mathsf{VF}(\mathrm{F}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}))
.

Remark 4.22
.

An important consequence of Theorem
4.21
is that, when the base tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
admits negatives, there is a well-defined notion of Lie bracket of full Finsler vector fields. This is surprising since, as we will explain later, horizontal vector fields (which are related to Finsler vector fields) are not closed under the Lie bracket. In particular, the failure of the Lie bracket of two horizontal vector fields to be horizontal is measured by the curvature.

However, it is crucial to remember which tangent category the Lie bracket is defined in. The Lie bracket of full Finsler vector fields is induced by the Finsler tangent structure, while the Lie bracket under which horizontal vector fields fail to be closed is induced by the base tangent category.

One may expect that the two Lie brackets should agree, since there is a comparison morphism
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\pi_{q})\colon\mathbb{T}\Rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{F}}
between the two corresponding tangent structures. However, this is not the case, since this morphism of tangent structures induces a functor
\mathsf{VF}(\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathbb{T})\to\mathrm{F}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
from the tangent category with the ordinary tangent structure to the Finsler one, not the other way around. Finally, one should also observe that not every Finsler vector field is full.

5
Distributions and involutivity

In the previous section, we introduced vertical and Finsler vector fields, which correspond to sections of the vertical and of the Finsler bundle, respectively. It is often convenient to regard vertical vector fields not merely as sections of the vertical bundle but instead as maps with values directly in the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}E
of the total space
E
of
q\colon E\to M
. A similar situation happens in the presence of a (horizontal) connection
\mathrm{H}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}E
which embeds the Finsler bundle into the tangent bundle and allows us to regard Finsler vector fields as maps with values in
\mathrm{T}E
. This perspective is facilitated by the concept of
distributions
.

In differential geometry, a
distribution
over an object
E
consists of a vector sub-bundle
D\subseteq\mathrm{T}E
of the tangent bundle of
E
. In this section, we generalize this concept to tangent categories, and prove an important property of the vertical bundle: as a distribution, the vertical bundle is
involutive
. Concretely, this means that vertical vector fields are closed under the Lie bracket associated to the tangent bundle of
E
. We begin by formally introducing the concept of a distribution.

Definition 5.1
.

In a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, a
tangent distribution
on
M
consists of a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon D\to M
together with a morphism
\iota\colon D\to\mathrm{T}M
subject to the following conditions:

​[TD.1]:

\iota
is tangent monic, that is, for every
n\geq 0
,
\mathrm{T}^{n}\iota
is monic;

​[TD.2]:

\iota\colon\mathbf{q}\to\mathbf{p}_{M}
is a linear morphism of differential bundles over
M
.

Given a tangent distribution (
\mathbf{q}\colon D\to M,\iota)
of
M
, a
(\mathbf{q},\iota)
-vector field
is a vector field
X\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
of
M
such that there exists a (necessarily unique) section
\hat{X}\colon M\to D
of the projection
q\colon D\to M
of the distribution, satisfying
X=\hat{X}\iota
.

Thanks to Lemma
2.18
, the vertical bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}\colon\mathrm{V}q\to E
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is a tangent distribution on
E
, where the inclusion map is
\iota_{q}
.

Definition 5.2
.

The
vertical distribution
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is the tangent distribution
(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}},\iota_{q})
, where
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
denotes the vertical bundle of
\mathbf{q}
and
\iota_{q}
the inclusion of
\mathrm{V}q
into
\mathrm{T}E
. We shall denote this distribution simply by
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
.

Using the language of distributions, vertical vector fields correspond precisely to
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
-vector fields. We now introduce the involutivity condition.

Definition 5.3
.

In a tangent category with negatives, a tangent distribution
(\mathbf{q},\iota)
is
involutive
provided that
\mathbf{q}
-vector fields form a Lie subalgebra
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};\mathbf{q},\iota)
of the Lie algebra
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};M)
of vector fields. Concretely, for every pair of sections
\hat{X},\hat{Y}\colon M\to D
of the projection
q\colon D\to M
, there exists a (necessarily unique) section
\hat{Z}\colon M\to D
satisfying the following equation

\displaystyle[\hat{X}\iota,\hat{Y}\iota]=\hat{Z}\iota

where
[,]
denotes the Lie bracket of the tangent bundle
\mathbf{p}_{M}
.

Not only is the vertical bundle a distribution, but crucially, it is also involutive. This is a consequence of Theorem
4.6
.

Corollary 5.4
.

In a tangent category with negatives, the vertical bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is an involutive distribution on
E
.

Proof.

One can prove this directly by showing that
[X,Y]\mathrm{T}q=qz_{M}
. Instead, we will give a more conceptual proof, which involves Theorem
4.6
. By this result, a vertical vector field
X\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
is equivalent to a vector field
\hat{X}\colon q\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q
on
q
in the slice tangent category. If
\hat{X}
and
\hat{Y}
denote the corresponding vector fields in the slice of two vertical vector fields
X
and
Y
, one can compute the Lie bracket of
\hat{X}
and
\hat{Y}
in the slice tangent category and obtain a vector field
[\hat{X},\hat{Y}]^{\mathrm{V}}\colon q\to\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{V}}q
.

Furthermore, each lax tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
lifts to a lax tangent morphism
\mathsf{VF}[F,\alpha]\colon\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
which sends every vector field
X
to
FX\alpha_{M}\colon FM\to\mathrm{T}^{\prime}FM
. In particular, the tangent morphism
\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of Lemma
2.13
lifts to a tangent morphism
\mathsf{VF}[\Pi]\colon\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M)\to\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. However, by Theorem
4.6
, the image of a vector field in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})/M
along
\Pi
is a vertical vector field.

Thus
\mathsf{VF}[\Pi]([\hat{X},\hat{Y}]^{\mathrm{V}})
is a vertical vector field of
q
. In particular,
\mathsf{VF}[\Pi]([\hat{X},\hat{Y}]^{\mathrm{V}})
corresponds to the vertical vector field
[\hat{X},\hat{Y}]^{\mathrm{V}}\iota_{q}
of
q
. However, for every lax tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)
,
\mathsf{VF}[F,\alpha]
preserves the Lie bracket. So, in particular, we obtain:

\displaystyle[\hat{X},\hat{Y}]^{\mathrm{V}}\iota_{q}=[\hat{X}\iota_{q},\hat{Y}\iota_{q}]=[X,Y]

proving that the Lie bracket
[X,Y]
of vertical vector fields
X
and
Y
is again a vertical vector field.
∎

The vertical bundle defines an involutive tangent distribution, and the corresponding
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
-vector fields are precisely the vertical vector fields. One might expect that Finsler bundles should give rise to
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
-vector fields. Unfortunately, the Finsler bundle is, in general, not a tangent distribution for
E
, let alone being involutive. In fact, a connection is exactly the mathematical device required to make
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
into a tangent distribution, the
horizontal distribution
. We shall start by proving that, given a horizontal connection,
(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}},\mathrm{H})
defines a tangent distribution.

Lemma 5.5
.

A horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}E
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
turns the Finsler bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}
of
q
into a tangent distribution of
E
.

Proof.

By
[HC.2]
,
\mathrm{H}
is a linear morphism; moreover, by
[HC.1]
,
\mathrm{H}
is a section of the horizontal descent of
q
. Thus,
\mathrm{H}
is tangent monic.
∎

Definition 5.6
.

The
horizontal distribution
of a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
equipped with a horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}
is the tangent distribution
(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}},\mathrm{H})
of Lemma
5.5
. We shall denote this distribution by
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{H}}
.

As vertical vector fields correspond to
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}
-vector fields, so horizontal vector fields correspond to
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{H}}
-vector fields.

6
Curvature and parallel transport

In this final section, we discuss how to define and work with curvature and parallel transport for an Erhesmann connection in a tangent category.

6.1
The curvature of an Ehresmann connection

Corollary
5.4
proves that the vertical distribution is involutive, that is, vertical vector fields are closed under the Lie bracket, and they form a Lie subalgebra of the Lie algebra of ordinary vector fields. One may hope that the horizontal distribution also satisfies the involutivity condition. However, this is not the case. In fact, the failure of horizontal vector fields to be closed under Lie bracket is measured by an important geometric quantity, known as the
curvature
of the connection. In this section, we introduce this concept in the context of tangent categories and prove that the exterior derivative of the curvature vanishes for horizontal vector fields, giving a Bianchi identity for this notion of curvature.

However, instead of directly defining the curvature as a measure of the failure of the horizontal distribution to be involutive, we shall take a different route, which enlightens the categorical nature of tangent categories. We will prove later on that our definition agrees with the classical notion. However, we begin with a philosophical detour to justify our intuition.

From a very general point of view, a
categorical
theory of a certain class of objects aims to provide the minimal setting in which such an object may be defined. For instance, categorically, a group consists of an object
G
in a Cartesian category
\mathbb{X}
, equipped with three maps
e\colon\ast\to G
,
\mu\colon G\times G\to G
, and
\iota\colon G\to G
, axiomatizing the unit, the multiplication, and the inverse operation. This definition not only makes sense in the standard setting where
G
is a set, but it also applies to new contexts, including the category of group objects
\mathsf{Grp}(\mathbb{X})
of a Cartesian category
\mathbb{X}
, itself.

It turns out that a group object in the category of group objects consists of an object
G
in the original category together with two group structures, which, however, must necessarily coincide and make
G
into an
Abelian
group. This is not a special feature of group objects, but rather a general phenomenon: a type of structure in the category of those structures is two of these structures with a comparison law between them. For example, a monoid in monoids is a commutative monoid and a monad in the
2
-category of monads consists of two monads over the same object together with a distributive law between those monads
[
20
]
.

Even in the context of tangent categories, this phenomenon appears for vector fields: a vector field in the tangent category of vector fields is a pair of commutative vector fields, that is, a pair
X,Y\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
of vector fields, satisfying
[X,Y]=0
.

Our proposed definition for the curvature makes use of the following idea. A non-flat connection
fails
to be a morphism of connections, and the obstruction is measured by the curvature. This idea was inspired by the definition of the curvature of a
Koszul connection

\mathsf{K}\colon\mathrm{T}E\to E
on a differential bundle
\mathbf{q}\colon E\to M
, proposed in
[
3
, Definition 3.20]
, given by the formula:

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{\mathsf{K}}=c_{E}\mathrm{T}\mathsf{K}\mathsf{K}-\mathrm{T}\mathsf{K}\mathsf{K}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}E\to E

From this expression, we have noticed that a Koszul connection
\mathsf{K}
is in fact flat, that is, the curvature vanishes, if and only if
\mathsf{K}\colon(\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q},c_{E}\mathrm{T}\mathsf{K})\to(\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q},c_{E}\mathrm{T}\mathsf{K})
is a morphism of Koszul connections, where
c_{E}\mathrm{T}\mathsf{K}
turns out to be a Koszul connection on
\mathrm{T}\mathbf{q}
.

To make this idea precise, we need to modify slightly the context we are working on. Given a tangent display submersion
q\colon E\to M
, equipped with an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
, the fundamental short exact sequence of
q
is the sequence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{q}}

Thanks to the properties of the vertical and the Finsler bundle, one can apply the tangent bundle functor and obtain a new short exact sequence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{0}_{E})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{p}_{E})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{0}_{E})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\iota_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\pi_{q}}

Notice that this is not the fundamental short exact sequence of
\mathrm{T}q
, because the central term is
\mathrm{T}\mathbf{p}_{E}
instead of
\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{T}E}
. One might also argue that,
\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}})
and
\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}})
are not the vertical and the Finsler bundle of
\mathrm{T}q
, since, by notation, they should be
(\mathrm{T}q)^{\mathrm{V}}
and
(\mathrm{T}q)^{\mathrm{F}}
, instead. However, one should remember that the vertical bundle and the Finsler bundle of a tangent display map are only specified up to a unique isomorphism (Remarks
2.17
and
2.22
), since they are defined by a choice of tangent pullbacks. This means that
\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}})
and
\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}})
are, in fact, different, but legitimate representations of the vertical and the Finsler bundles of
\mathrm{T}q
. With that in mind, we can use the canonical flip to make the central term, back to
\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{T}E}
, obtaining a correct representation of the fundamental short exact sequence of
\mathrm{T}q
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm{T}E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{p}_{\mathrm{T}E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{F}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm{T}E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\iota_{q}c_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{E}\mathrm{T}\pi_{q}}

With this choice of the vertical and the Finsler bundles, an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
on
q
, defines a new Ehresmann connection on
\mathrm{T}q
given by
(c_{E}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{R},\mathrm{T}\mathrm{H}c_{E})
. Notice that with the standard choice of the vertical and the Finsler bundle, that is,
(\mathrm{T}q)^{\mathrm{V}}
and
(\mathrm{T}q)^{\mathrm{F}}
, the corresponding Ehresmann connection on
\mathrm{T}q
would be
(c_{E}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{R}\gamma^{\mathrm{V}},\gamma^{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{H}c_{E})
, instead. Unfortunately, this choice does not work for our purposes, which is why we need to work with
\mathrm{T}(\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{V}})
instead of
(\mathrm{T}q)^{\mathrm{V}}
.

It is easy to work with the connection forms, instead of directly with the vertical and the horizontal connections. So, in particular,
(c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi,\mathrm{T}\psi c_{E})
defines an Ehresmann connection form for
\mathrm{T}q
.

Now, recall that given two tangent display maps
q\colon E\to M
and
q^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to M
over the same object
M
, respectively equipped with a vertical connection form
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
, a
morphism of vertical connection forms
consists of a morphism
f\colon E\to E^{\prime}
that commutes with
q
and
q^{\prime}
, that is,
fq^{\prime}=q
, and commutes with the connection forms; that is,
\mathrm{T}f\phi^{\prime}=\phi\mathrm{T}f
.

We are now in the position to define the concepts of flatness and of curvature of an Ehresmann connection.

Definition 6.1
.

An Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is
flat
provided that the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi}

(6.1)

commutes, where
\phi
and
\psi
denote the vertical and the horizontal connection forms of the connection.

We may now give a fully categorical characterization of flatness.

Proposition 6.2
.

Consider a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
. An Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
is flat if and only if the horizontal connection form
\psi
defines a morphism of vertical connection forms
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\psi)\colon(\mathrm{T}q,c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi)\to(\mathrm{T}q,c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi)
.

Proof.

Let us start by assuming that the connection is flat. From Lemma
3.9
, we compute that
\psi\mathrm{T}q=\pi_{q}\mathrm{H}\mathrm{T}q=\pi_{q}\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{F}}q=\mathrm{T}q
. Thus,
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\psi)\colon\mathrm{T}q\to\mathrm{T}q
defines a morphism of tangent display maps. Moreover, the flatness condition establishes precisely that
\mathrm{T}\psi c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi=c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi\mathrm{T}\psi
, that is, that
(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}M},\psi)
is a morphism of vertical connection forms. Conversely, the latter condition is precisely flatness.
∎

When the tangent category admits negatives, one can measure the failure of a connection to be flat by quantifying the failure of the diagram of Equation (
6.1
) to commute.

Definition 6.3
.

The (
categorical
)
curvature
of an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
in a tangent category with negatives is the morphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi\mathrm{T}\psi-\mathrm{T}\psi c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}E\to\mathrm{T}^{2}E

Remark 6.4
.

Notice that the formula that defines the curvature of an Ehresmann connection involves both the vertical and the horizontal connection. However, as extensively discussed in the previous sections, with negatives, one is fully determined by the other. Therefore, it is natural to wonder if one may express the curvature entirely using the vertical or the horizontal component of the connection. This is possible, thanks to
[ECF.2]
, which establishes that
\phi+\psi=\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}
. In particular, this allows us to write
\phi
as
\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}-\psi
and viceversa,
\psi=\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}-\phi
, when negatives are available. After some obvious computations, one obtains the following two formulas:

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=p_{\mathrm{T}E}\mathrm{T}z_{E}-c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi+\mathrm{T}\phi c\mathrm{T}\phi

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=p_{\mathrm{T}E}\mathrm{T}z_{E}-\mathrm{T}\psi c_{E}+\mathrm{T}\psi c_{E}\mathrm{T}\psi

We will refer to
\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{R}}
and
\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{H}}
as the
vertical curvature
of a vertical connection and the
horizontal curvature
of a horizontal connection. When
\mathrm{R}
and
\mathrm{H}
are the vertical and the horizontal components of an Ehresmann connection, then
\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{R}}=\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}=\mathsf{C}_{\mathrm{H}}
.

To our knowledge, the definition of curvature does not exist in the literature in this form. In fact, this definition relies on the categorical nature of tangent categories: the categorical curvature measures the failure of the diagram (
6.1
) to commute. This formulation of the curvature is
intrinsic
since it does not rely on the language of tensors or differential forms. Pictorially, we can represent the curvature as a
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi}

that measures the failure of the horizontal connection form to be a morphism of vertical connection forms. In the following, we shall assume that the ambient tangent category admits negatives.

Lemma 6.5
.

The curvature
\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}
of an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}=p_{\mathrm{T}E}\mathrm{T}z_{E}-\mathrm{T}\psi c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi

Proof.

The proof follows immediately from the following computation. Recall that, from
[ECF.2]
, we can write
\psi
as
\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}-\phi
. Thus, using the fact that
\phi
is an idempotent, we shall compute that

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\phi\mathrm{T}\psi=\mathrm{T}\phi(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}^{2}E}\ominus\mathrm{T}\phi)=\mathrm{T}\phi\ominus\mathrm{T}\phi^{2}=\mathrm{T}\phi\ominus\mathrm{T}\phi=\mathrm{T}p_{E}\mathrm{T}z_{E}

where
\ominus
denotes the difference operator induced by
\mathrm{T}s_{E}
and
\mathrm{T}n_{E}
(in contrast with
-
which is the difference operator induced by
s_{\mathrm{T}E}
and
n_{\mathrm{T}E}
). From this computation, it is immediate to rewrite
c_{E}\mathrm{T}\psi\mathrm{T}\phi
as
p_{\mathrm{T}E}\mathrm{T}z_{E}
, since
c_{E}\mathrm{T}p_{E}=p_{\mathrm{T}E}
.
∎

Thanks to the previous lemma, we shall now prove that the curvature precisely measures the failure of the horizontal distribution to be involutive.

Theorem 6.6
.

Consider a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
equipped with an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
. Given two horizontal vector fields
X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
, the following formula holds:

\displaystyle\left[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}\right]_{\mathrm{V}}=\left\{Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\right\}

where
Z_{\mathrm{V}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=Z\phi
denotes the vertical component of a vector field
Z\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
. Moreover, if the connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
is flat, the horizontal distribution
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{H}}
is involutive.

Proof.

Consider two horizontal vector fields
X_{\mathrm{H}}
and
Y_{\mathrm{H}}
. By definition, the Lie bracket of
X_{\mathrm{H}}
and
Y_{\mathrm{H}}
can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\left[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}\right]=\left\{X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}Y_{\mathrm{H}}-Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}c_{E}\right\}

Now, using the properties of the bracket
\{-\}
proved in
[
1
, Lemma 2.14]
,
\{f\}\phi=\{f\mathrm{T}\phi\}
, provided that the two sides of the equation are well-defined. However, when
\phi
is linear, both the two sides are defined provided that
\{f\}
is. Therefore

\displaystyle\left[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}\right]_{\mathrm{V}}=\left[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}\right]\phi=\left\{X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}Y_{\mathrm{H}}-Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}c_{E}\right\}\phi=\left\{\left(X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}Y_{\mathrm{H}}-Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}c_{E}\right)\mathrm{T}\phi\right\}=

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\{X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}\phi-Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi\right\}

where, in the last step, we used the naturality of
s
and
n
to distribute
\mathrm{T}\phi
. However, since by Corollary
4.13

X_{\mathrm{H}}=X_{\mathrm{H}}\psi
and
Y_{\mathrm{H}}=Y_{\mathrm{H}}\psi
, we can also write:

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}\phi-Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi=X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}\psi\mathrm{T}\phi-Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}\psi c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi

By
[ECF.1]
,
\psi\phi=p_{E}z_{E}
, therefore, we can rewrite the first term as follows

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}\psi\mathrm{T}\phi=X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}p_{E}\mathrm{T}z_{E}=X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}(Y_{\mathrm{H}}p_{E})\mathrm{T}z_{E}=X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}z_{E}

where we used that
Y_{\mathrm{H}}
is a vector field and therefore,
Y_{\mathrm{H}}p_{E}=\mathsf{id}_{E}
. Now, the second term can be rearranged using Lemma
6.5
, as follows:

\displaystyle-Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}\psi c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi=Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}(p_{\mathrm{T}E}\mathrm{T}z_{E}-\mathrm{T}\psi c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi-p_{\mathrm{T}E}\mathrm{T}z_{E})=Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}(\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}-p_{\mathrm{T}E}\mathrm{T}z_{E})=

\displaystyle\qquad=Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}-Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}p_{\mathrm{T}E}\mathrm{T}z_{E}

However, using the naturality of
p
and that
Y_{\mathrm{H}}
is a vector field, we can also write the last term as follows:

\displaystyle Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}p_{\mathrm{T}E}\mathrm{T}z_{E}=Y_{\mathrm{H}}p_{E}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}z_{E}=X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}z_{E}

Putting all the pieces together, we finally compute:

\displaystyle\left[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}\right]_{\mathrm{V}}=\left\{X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}\psi\mathrm{T}\phi-Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}\psi c_{E}\mathrm{T}\phi\right\}=\left\{X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}z_{E}+Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}-X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}z_{E}\right\}

The terms
X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}z_{E}
cancel each other out, leaving us with the desired formula:

\displaystyle\left[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}\right]_{\mathrm{V}}=\left\{Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\right\}

Finally, if the connection is flat,
\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}
vanishes, that is,
[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}}=0
. This means that the vertical component of the Lie bracket
[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]
of two horizontal vector fields vanishes. However, thanks to Proposition
4.14
, a vector field
Z\colon E\to\mathrm{T}E
can be uniquely decomposed into the sum
Z_{\mathrm{V}}+Z_{\mathrm{H}}
of a vertical vector field
Z_{\mathrm{V}}
and a horizontal vector field
Z_{\mathrm{H}}
. Therefore, if the vertical component
[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}}
vanishes, means that
[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]
is necessarily horizontal, that is, the horizontal distribution is involutive.
∎

In general, requiring the horizontal distribution to be involutive does not suffice to show that the connection is flat. However, this implication holds when every morphism
f\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}E\to N
is uniquely determined by the assignment

\displaystyle\hat{f}\colon\mathsf{HVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};q)\times\mathsf{HVF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};q)\to\mathbb{X}(E,N)

\displaystyle\hat{f}(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}f

which sends a pair of horizontal vector fields to the map
Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}F
. In this case, we shall say that horizontal vector fields
separate
the morphisms of type
\mathrm{T}^{2}E\to N
.

Definition 6.7
.

In a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, horizontal vector fields
separate linear maps
provided that, given a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
equipped with a horizontal connection
\mathrm{H}
, for every
n\geq 0
, for every differential bundle
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
and every pair of linear morphisms
f,g\colon\mathrm{T}^{n}\mathbf{p}_{E}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
of differential bundles, the equations

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{H}}^{1}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}\dots\mathrm{T}^{n}X_{\mathrm{H}}^{n}f=X_{\mathrm{H}}^{1}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}\dots\mathrm{T}^{n}X_{\mathrm{H}}^{n}g

for every
n
-tuple
X_{\mathrm{H}}^{1},{\dots},X_{\mathrm{H}}^{n}
of horizontal vector fields, imply that
f=g
.

Corollary 6.8
.

In a tangent category with negatives where horizontal vector fields separate linear maps, an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is flat if and only if the horizontal distribution
\mathbf{q}^{\mathrm{H}}
is involutive.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.6
, we already know that if the connection is flat, the horizontal distribution is necessarily involutive. Now, assume that the horizontal distribution is involutive, that is, for every pair of horizontal vector fields
X_{\mathrm{H}}
and
Y_{\mathrm{H}}
, the Lie bracket
[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]
is again a horizontal vector field. From the decomposition of Proposition
4.14
, we conclude that the vertical component
[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}}
must vanish. Therefore, by the formula of Theorem
6.6
,
\{Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\}=0
. However, using the universal property of the vertical lift, this implies that
Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}
must vanish, that is,
Y_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}=z_{E}z_{\mathrm{T}E}
. However, since
X_{\mathrm{H}}
and
Y_{\mathrm{H}}
are vector fields,
z_{E}z_{\mathrm{T}E}=X_{\mathrm{H}}p_{E}Y_{\mathrm{H}}p_{E}z_{E}z_{\mathrm{T}E}z_{E}z_{\mathrm{T}E}
, which, by using the naturality of
p
, can be rewritten as
X_{\mathrm{H}}p_{E}Y_{\mathrm{H}}p_{E}z_{E}z_{\mathrm{T}E}=X_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{T}Y_{\mathrm{H}}p_{\mathrm{T}E}p_{E}z_{E}z_{\mathrm{T}E}
. Finally, by the separability condition, this implies that
\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}=p_{\mathrm{T}E}p_{E}z_{E}z_{\mathrm{T}E}
. Therefore, since the curvature vanishes, the connection is flat.
∎

The separability condition holds in important cases, such as in the tangent category of finite-dimensional smooth manifolds. This is one of the reasons why differential geometers prefer to work directly with the curvature tensor instead of with an intrinsic notion of curvature as in Definition
6.3
.

6.2
The structural equation and the Bianchi identity

An important identity proved in differential geometry, known as Cartan’s structural equation, used in the context of principal connections on principal bundles
[
8
]
, relates the curvature of a connection with the exterior differential of the connection. In this section, we extend this identity to the tangent-categorical context by introducing a proper notion of the exterior differential. We will use this identity to prove another important identity: the Bianchi identity. In gauge field theory, this identity establishes a local law of conservation of energy. We advise the interested reader to see
[
8
]
.

We may start by introducing the exterior differential. As before, let us assume the ambient tangent category has negatives.

Definition 6.9
.

A (
vector-valued
)
differential form of rank
n
on an object
E
consists of a function

\displaystyle\omega\colon\mathsf{VF}_{n}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)\to\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)

where
\mathsf{VF}_{n}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)\times{\dots}\times\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
and
n\geq 1
. Furthermore,
\omega
is required to be
additive in each variable
, that is,

\displaystyle\omega(X_{1},{\dots},0,{\dots},X_{n})=0

\displaystyle\omega(X_{1},{\dots},X_{i}+Y_{i},{\dots},X_{n})=\omega(X_{1},{\dots},X_{i},{\dots},X_{n})+\omega(X_{1},{\dots},Y_{i},{\dots},X_{n})

and
alternating
, that is

\displaystyle\omega(X_{\sigma(1)},{\dots},X_{\sigma(n)})=(-)^{\sigma}\omega(X_{1},{\dots},X_{n})

where
(-)^{\sigma}
denotes the sign of the permutation
\sigma
on
n
indices.

We may define the
exterior derivative
of a differential form
\omega
by the following formula

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathsf{d}\omega\colon\mathsf{VF}_{n+1}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)\to\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)\\
&\mathsf{d}\omega(X_{1},{\dots},X_{n+1})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}(-)^{k+1}\left[X_{k},\omega\left(X_{1},{\dots},\hat{X}_{k},{\dots},X_{n+1}\right)\right]+\\
&\qquad+\sum_{i<j}(-)^{i+j}\omega\left(\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right],X_{1},{\dots},\hat{X}_{i},{\dots},\hat{X}_{j},{\dots},X_{n+1}\right)\end{split}

(6.2)

where
\hat{X}_{k}
indicates the omission of that vector field.

Remark 6.10
.

Formula (
6.2
) extends to vector-valued differential forms the
invariant formula
of the exterior derivative of ordinary differential forms of a smooth manifold, as in
[
13
, Proposition 14.32]
. In its original formulation for ordinary differential forms, the Lie bracket
[X_{k},\omega(\dots)]
in the first sum, are replaced by the Lie derivative
\mathscr{L}_{X}\omega
.

Theorem 6.11
.

The exterior differential of vector-valued differential forms squares to zero, that is, the following formula

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\mathsf{d}\omega=0

holds for every vector-valued differential form
\omega
.

Proof.

Despite being a standard result in differential geometry known as the Poincaré lemma, in our setting, we lack a local expression for differential forms, since in a general tangent category, there is no well-defined notion of local charts. Unfortunately, this identity is usually proved using the local form of differential forms, which makes it an easy exercise. It is not as trivial when using the invariant formula we have used here. Therefore, we will give a full proof of this result. We start by introducing some handy notation that will simplify the computations.

For starters, we shall denote by
\Altsum_{i}a_{i}
, an alternating sum of terms
a_{1},{\dots},a_{n}
, that is, the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-)^{i+1}a_{i}
. This notation will be useful when we need to change the meaning of the index
i
, while keeping track of the alternating sign. Secondly, we shall denote by
\vec{X}
a tuple of vector fields
X_{1},{\dots},X_{n}
and by
\vec{X}_{\hat{i}_{1},{\dots},\hat{i}_{k}}
the tuple
X_{1},{\dots},\hat{X}_{i_{1}},{\dots},\hat{X}_{i_{k}},{\dots},X_{n}
, where the
i_{1}
-th-
1_{k}
-th terms have been removed from
\vec{X}
. To become comfortable with this notation, we shall start by rewriting the defining formula of the exterior derivative for an arbitrary differential form
\theta
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\theta\left(\vec{X}\right)=\Altsum_{k}\left[X_{k},\theta\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{k}}\right)\right]+\sum_{i<j}\left(-\right)^{i+j}\theta\left(\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right],\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)

Therefore, with this notation,
\mathsf{d}\mathsf{d}\omega
can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\mathsf{d}\omega\left(\vec{X}\right)=\Altsum_{k}\left[X_{k},\mathsf{d}\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{k}}\right)\right]+\sum_{i<j}\left(-\right)^{i+j}\mathsf{d}\omega\left(\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right],\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)

(6.3)

To avoid getting trapped in a long, complicated chain of identities, we split the right-hand side into two pieces. We shall start by unwrapping the first bit:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Altsum_{k}\left[X_{k},\mathsf{d}\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{k}}\right)\right]&=\underbracket{\Altsum_{k}\left[X_{k},\Altsum_{k^{\prime}\neq k}\left[X_{k^{\prime}},\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{k},\hat{k}^{\prime}}\right)\right]\right]}_{\text{(1)}}+\\
&+\underbracket{\Altsum_{k}\left[X_{k},\sum_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime};i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\neq k}\left(-\right)^{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}}\omega\left(\left[X_{i^{\prime}},X_{j^{\prime}}\right],\vec{X}_{\hat{i}^{\prime},\hat{j}^{\prime},\hat{k}}\right)\right]}_{\text{(2)}}\end{split}

(6.4)

Let us have a closer look at the term (1). If we want to express the alternating sum as an actual sum, we need to split the internal sum into two pieces, as follows:

\displaystyle(1)=\sum_{k=2}^{n+2}(-)^{k+1}\left[X_{k},\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k-1}(-)^{k^{\prime}+1}[X_{k^{\prime}},\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{k}^{\prime},\hat{k}}\right)\right]-\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}(-)^{k+1}\left[X_{k},\sum_{k^{\prime}=k+1}^{n+2}(-)^{k^{\prime}+1}[X_{k^{\prime}},\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{k}^{\prime},\hat{k}}\right)\right]

where the negative sign between the two sums comes from shifting the alternating sum in
k^{\prime}
to one position, since
k^{\prime}\neq k
. Now, using the additivity of the Lie bracket, and after renaming the indices
k\mapsto i
and
k^{\prime}\mapsto j
, we rearrange the two terms as follows:

\displaystyle(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j<i}(-)^{i+j}\left[X_{i},[X_{j},\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)\right]-\sum_{i<j}(-)^{i+j}\left[X_{i},\left[X_{j},\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)\right]\right]

However, in the first term, we can swap the two indices by virtue of the identity
\sum_{j<i}=\sum_{i=2}^{n+2}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\sum_{i=j+1}^{n+2}
. Therefore, after renaming the indices
i\mapsto j
,
j\mapsto i
, we rewrite the terms as follows:

\displaystyle(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i<j}(-)^{i+j}\left[X_{j},[X_{i},\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)\right]-\sum_{i<j}(-)^{i+j}\left[X_{i},[X_{j},\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)\right]

(6.5)

Let us now come back to Equation (
6.3
), and let us take a closer look at the second sum. In particular, since the first argument of
\mathsf{d}\omega
is
[X_{i},X_{j}]
, it is useful to separate the first term from the rest of the alternating sums that define
\mathsf{d}\omega
, as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{i<j}\left(-\right)^{i+j}\mathsf{d}\omega\left(\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right],\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbracket{\sum_{i<j}(-)^{i+j}\left[\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right],\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)\right]}_{\text{(A)}}+

\displaystyle-\underbracket{\sum_{i<j}(-)^{i+j}\Altsum_{k^{\prime}\neq i,j}\left[X_{k^{\prime}},\omega\left(\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right],\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j},\hat{k}^{\prime}}\right)\right]}_{\text{(B)}}+

\displaystyle+\underbracket{\sum_{i<j}(-)^{i+j}\sum_{j^{\prime}\neq i,j}(-)^{j^{\prime}+1}\omega\left(\left[\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right],X_{j^{\prime}}\right],\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j},\hat{j}^{\prime}}\right)}_{\text{(C)}}+

\displaystyle-\underbracket{\sum_{i<j}(-)^{i+j}\sum_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime};i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\neq i,j}(-)^{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}}\omega\left(\left[X_{i^{\prime}},X_{j^{\prime}}\right],\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right],\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{i}^{\prime},\hat{j},\hat{j}^{\prime}}\right)}_{\text{(D)}}

Crucially, the terms (B) and (D) carry a negative sign due to the shift in the alternating sum. After simple arrangements, it is not hard to see that the terms (2) of Equation (
6.4
) and (B) cancel each other out. Furthermore, the term (C) contains the expressions
[[X_{i},X_{j}],X_{j^{\prime}}]
, where
i<j
and
j^{\prime}\neq i,j
. Let us restrict first to the case when
j^{\prime}<i
. Then,
j^{\prime}<i<j
. Therefore, within the sum, for each of such triple of indices
j^{\prime}<i<j
, there will be the terms
[[X_{j^{\prime}},X_{i}],X_{j}]
,
[[X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}],X_{i}]
, and
[[X_{i},X_{j}],X_{j^{\prime}}]
. However, by the Jacobi identity, the sum of these three terms vanishes. The same exact situation happens when
i<j^{\prime}<j
and when
i<j<j^{\prime}
, and moreover, every term in the sum of (C) is of one of these forms. Therefore, by the Jacobi identity used multiple times, the term (C) vanishes entirely. The term (D) also vanishes by antisymmetry. In fact, within the sum, the pairs of indices
(i,j)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
swap, and therefore, by antisymmetry of the Lie bracket, the whole sum must vanish. The only remaining terms are (1) of Equation (
6.4
) and (A). Using Equation (
6.5
), we can write the sum (1)+(A) as follows:

\displaystyle\text{(1)}+\text{(A)}=\sum_{j<i}(-)^{i+j}\left(\left[X_{j},\left[X_{i},\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)\right]\right]-\left[X_{i},[X_{j},\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)\right]+\left[\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right],\omega\left(\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}}\right)\right]\right)

Let us call
X\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=X_{i}
,
Y\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=X_{j}
, and
Z\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\omega(\vec{X}_{\hat{i},\hat{j}})
. Thus, the term in the brackets reads as follows:

\displaystyle[Y,[X,Z]]-[X,[Y,Z]]+[[X,Y],Z]=[Y,[X,Z]]+[Z,[X,Y]]+[Y,[Z,X]]+[[X,Y],Z]=0

where in the first step we used the Jacobi identity and the latter, the antisymmetry. This proves that
\mathsf{d}\mathsf{d}\omega=0
.
∎

The vertical connection form
\phi
of an Ehresmann connection defines a differential form

\displaystyle\phi\colon\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)\to\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)

\displaystyle\phi(X)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=X_{\mathrm{V}}=X\phi

that sends every vector field on the total space
E
to its vertical component.

Definition 6.12
.

The
curvature form
of an Ehresmann connection
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
is the vector-valued differential form defined by the following formula

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\colon\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)\times\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)\to\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}(X,Y)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathsf{d}\phi(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}})

where, as usual,
X_{\mathrm{H}}=X\psi
and
Y_{\mathrm{H}}=Y\psi
denote the horizontal components of the vector fields
X
and
Y
, respectively.

The relation between the curvature differential form and the categorical curvature is expressed by the structural equation. To compare this identity with the existing literature, we need to introduce a suitable notion of the wedge product between vector-valued differential forms. To this end, consider two differential forms
\omega
and
\tau
of rank
n
and
m
, respectively. Then,
[\omega\wedge\tau]
is the differential form of rank
n+m
defined by the following formula:

\displaystyle[\omega\wedge\tau](X_{1},{\dots},X_{n+m})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{n!m!}\sum_{\sigma}(-)^{\sigma}\left[\omega\left(X_{\sigma(1)},{\dots},X_{\sigma(n)}\right),\tau\left(X_{\sigma(n+1)},{\dots},X_{\sigma(n+m)}\right)\right]

The reader may be bothered by the factor
1/{n!m!}
, which is not well-defined in our context. However, this is simply a factor that keeps track of the repetitions that appear when expanding the whole formula, due to the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket and the alternating property of the differential forms. In practice, once the formula is fully expanded, no fractional factor is multiplied to the term. To match the notation used in the diffential geometry literature, we shall use the following identities:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\phi\wedge\phi](X,Y)=[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]

\displaystyle[\phi\wedge\psi]_{\mathrm{V}}(X,Y)=[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}}+[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]_{\mathrm{V}}

Notice that, when the commutative monoid
\mathsf{VF}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};E)
of vector fields over
E
has characteristic distinct from
2
, that is,
2X=0
implies
X=0
, then
1/2[\phi\wedge\phi]
is the differential form uniquely determined by the equation
2(1/2[\phi\wedge\phi])(X,Y)=2[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]
.

Theorem 6.13

(Structural equation)
.

The following identities hold:

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}(X,Y)=-[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}=\mathsf{d}\phi+\frac{1}{2}[\phi\wedge\phi]-\left([\phi\wedge\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}]-[\phi\wedge\psi]_{\mathrm{V}}\right)

Proof.

We shall start by unwrapping the definition of the exterior derivative of the connection form
\phi
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ [X,Y_{\mathrm{V}}]-[Y,X_{\mathrm{V}}]-[X,Y]_{\mathrm{V}}

(
Formula

(
6.2
)
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ [X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]+[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]+[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]+[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]+

\displaystyle\penalty\ \qquad-[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]_{\mathrm{V}}-[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}}-[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]_{\mathrm{V}}-[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}}

(
Corollary

3.23
)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ [X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]+[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{H}}+[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]_{\mathrm{H}}-[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}}

(
[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]\text{ is vertical}
)

When we take
X
and
Y
as horizontal vector fields, the only term that survives is
-[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}}
. Therefore, the curvature
\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}(X,Y)
is equal to
-[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}}
. Let us now use the wedge product of vector-valued differential forms to express the remaining terms. By using the defining formula we obtain:

\displaystyle[\phi\wedge\phi](X,Y)=[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]-[Y_{\mathrm{V}},X_{\mathrm{V}}]=2[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]

\displaystyle[\phi\wedge\psi](X,Y)=[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]-[Y_{\mathrm{V}},X_{\mathrm{H}}]=[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]+[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]

Using the bilinearity of the wedge product and
[EC.2]
, we can also write:

\displaystyle[\phi\wedge\psi]=[\phi\wedge\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}]-[\phi\wedge\phi]=[\phi\wedge\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}]_{\mathrm{V}}+[\phi\wedge\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}]_{\mathrm{H}}-[\phi\wedge\phi]

\displaystyle\quad=[\phi\wedge\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}]_{\mathrm{V}}+[\phi\wedge\phi]_{\mathrm{H}}+[\phi\wedge\psi]_{\mathrm{H}}-[\phi\wedge\phi]

However,
[\phi\wedge\phi]_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)=2[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]_{\mathrm{H}}
is necessarily zero, since the vertical distribution is involutive. Thus, we obtain that:

\displaystyle[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]+[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]=([\phi\wedge\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}]_{\mathrm{V}}+[\phi\wedge\phi]_{\mathrm{H}}+-[\phi\wedge\phi])(X,Y)

Putting everything together, we obtain the desired formula.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

In the context of principal connections over principal bundles in the differential geometry literature, the second identity of Theorem
6.13
is known as
Cartan’s structural equation
, and takes the following simplified form:

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}=\mathsf{d}\phi+\frac{1}{2}[\phi\wedge\phi]

In particular, using
[
8
, Lemma 5.5.5]
and the fact that Lie derivatives of fundamental vector fields vanish, the term
[\phi\wedge\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}]-[\phi\wedge\psi]_{\mathrm{V}}
disappears.

Remark 6.15
.

In the differential geometry literature, the structural equation for the curvature form of an Ehresmann connection can also be expressed in terms of the Frölicher-Nijenhuis bracket
[-,-]^{\mathsf{FN}}
and takes the form
[
11
, Section 17]
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}=\frac{1}{2}[\phi,\phi]^{\mathsf{FN}}

We decided not to investigate this operation in this paper and instead express the structural equations in relation to the exterior derivative.

We can finally now prove a version of the Bianchi identity in the context of Ehresmann connections in a tangent category.

Theorem 6.16

(Bianchi identity)
.

The following identity holds:

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{d}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}+[\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}\wedge\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}]_{\mathrm{H}}\right)(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}})=0

Proof.

From the structural equation and using that the exterior derivative squares to zero, we can reduce the derivative of the curvature form to the following expression

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}=\mathsf{d}\left(\mathsf{d}\phi+\theta\right)=\mathsf{d}\theta

where
\theta(X,Y)=[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]+[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{H}}+[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{V}}]_{\mathrm{H}}
. Notice that,
\theta(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}})=0
. Let us compute the exterior derivative of
\theta
explicitly. From the formula (
6.2
):

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\theta(X,Y,Z)=[X,\theta(Y,Z)]-[Y,\theta(X,Z)]+[Z,\theta(X,Y)]-\theta([X,Y],Z)+\theta([X,Z],Y)-\theta([Y,Z],X)

By taking
X,Y,Z
as horizontal vector fields and using that
\theta
vanishes for horizontal vector fields, we can reduce it to the following:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\theta(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}})=-\theta([X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}],Z_{\mathrm{H}})+\theta([X_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}}],Y_{\mathrm{H}})-\theta([Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}}],X_{\mathrm{H}})

However, for a vector field
X
and a horizontal vector field
Y_{\mathrm{H}}
,
\theta(X,Y_{\mathrm{H}})=[X_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{H}}
, since the other terms vanish. Therefore:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\theta(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}})=-[[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}},Z_{\mathrm{H}}]+[[X_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]-[[Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}},X_{\mathrm{H}}]

Let us now compute the wedge product
[\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\wedge\psi]
. By expanding the defining formula (notice that the factor
1/n!m!
handles the duplication due to the antisymmetry):

\displaystyle[\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\wedge\psi](X,Y,Z)=-[[X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}},Z_{\mathrm{H}}]-[[Z_{\mathrm{H}},X_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}},Y_{\mathrm{H}}]-[[Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}}]_{\mathrm{V}},X_{\mathrm{H}}]

Therefore, we obtain the formula:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\theta(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}})=[\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\wedge\psi]_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}})

We can also rewrite the right term as follows

\displaystyle[\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\wedge\psi]_{\mathrm{H}}=[\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\wedge\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}]_{\mathrm{H}}-[\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\wedge\phi]_{\mathrm{H}}=[\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\wedge\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}]_{\mathrm{H}}

where the term
[\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\wedge\phi]_{\mathrm{H}}
vanishes since
\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}(X,Y)
is vertical and the vertical distribution is involutive. This proves the final formula:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}})=[\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}\wedge\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}]_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}})

The right-hand side can be brought to the left as
[\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}\wedge\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}]_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}})
.
∎

Remark 6.17
.

For principal connections in differential geometry, the term
[\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}E}\wedge\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}]_{\mathrm{H}}
vanishes, leaving
\mathsf{d}\mathsf{C}_{(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})}(X_{\mathrm{H}},Y_{\mathrm{H}},Z_{\mathrm{H}})=0
.

6.3
Parallel transport

One of the most important consequences of the existence of a connection in differential geometry is the resulting parallel transport. Given a curve in the base space
M
of a bundle
q:E\to M
with a connection and a point “upstairs” (that is, in
E
), parallel transport allows one to move the curve upstairs, so that it starts at the given point, and stays “parallel” relative to the connection.

In differential geometry, the existence of parallel transport involves solving a certain ordinary differential equation. In tangent category theory, one needs to add additional structure to talk about ordinary differential equations and their solutions; such a structure is called a
curve object
.

In
[
3
]
, curve objects are first introduced. They allow one to talk about (ordinary) differential equations, and they assume that any solution to a differential equation is unique. They
do not
assume that every differential equation has a (total) solution, as this is not true in smooth manifolds (solutions may only exist locally, but not globally). The paper also considers
linearly complete
curve objects, which assume that
linear
differential equations always have (total) solutions (again, this is true in smooth manifolds).

With these assumptions,
[
3
, Theorem 5.20]
shows that every Koszul connection on a differential bundle in a tangent category with a linearly complete curve object gives rise to parallel transport in that bundle. This is achieved by associating to any Koszul connection a linear differential equation, whose (unique) solution gives rise to the required parallel transport.

However, the situation is slightly different for connections on a (not necessarily differential/vector) bundle. In this case, the resulting vector field one can build from the connection is not necessarily linear, so a (total) solution to it may not exist.

In this section, we describe what this means for a connection in a Cartesian tangent category with a curve object. We show that given a connection, one can build an associated vector field. We then show that
if
the vector field has a total solution (which may not always be the case), then the resulting solution gives a notion of parallel transport for the connection. The proofs themselves follow almost identically to their versions in the differential bundle case.

We leave it to future work to describe tangent categories whose curve objects have partial solutions to vector fields; in this case we anticipate that every connection in such a setting would give rise to a partially-defined parallel transport (as is true in smooth manifolds).

We begin by recalling dynamical systems and curve objects in a tangent category.

Definition 6.18
.

A
dynamical system
on an object
M
consists of a map
s\colon N\to M
(which represents the
initial condition
parametrized by
N
) and a vector field
X\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
.

We may denote a dynamical system by
(M,s,X)
.

Definition 6.19
.

A
curve object
in a Cartesian tangent category is a dynamical system
(C,c_{0},c_{1})
where
c_{0}\colon\ast\to C
which has the following three properties:

​[CO.1]:

Uniqueness of solutions
. For any dynamical system
(M,s,X)
, there is at most one map
\gamma\colon C\times N\to M
such that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(C\times N)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle!c_{0},\mathsf{id}_{N}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}\times z_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

When it exists, the map
\gamma
is called a
solution
3
3

3

For details on how this can be seen as a solution to a differential equation, see
[
5
, Section 3.2]
of the system
(M,s,X)
.

​[CO.2]:

Commutativity of
c_{1}
.
c_{1}T(c_{1})c=c_{1}T(c_{1})
.

​[CO.3]:

Completeness of
c_{1}
. The system
(C,c_{0},c_{1})
has a solution.

Remark 6.20
.

We will not need Conditions
[CO.1]
and
[CO.2]
for parallel transport.

For the rest of this section, we work in a Cartesian tangent category, which is a tangent category with finite products (including the terminal object) preserved by the tangent bundle functor, with a fixed curve object
(C,c_{0},c_{1})
.

Lemma 6.21
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{H}\colon\mathrm{F}q\to\mathrm{T}E
is a horizontal connection on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
,
\gamma\colon C\to M
is a map, and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\ast}(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

is a pullback. Then there is a vector field
\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}\colon\gamma^{\ast}(E)\to\mathrm{T}(\gamma^{\ast}(E))
on
\gamma^{\ast}(E)
, defined by

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\langle\pi_{0}c_{1},\langle\pi_{0}c_{1}\mathrm{T}(\gamma),\pi_{1}\rangle\mathrm{H}\rangle

Proof.

That this is a well-defined vector field is exactly the same as the first parts of the proof of Theorem 5.20 in
[
3
]
.
∎

Theorem 6.22
.

(Parallel transport) Suppose that
(\mathrm{R},\mathrm{H})
is an Ehresmann connection on a tangent display map
q\colon E\to M
,
\gamma\colon C\to M
is a map,
e_{0}\colon\ast\to M
is a map such that
e_{0}q=c_{0}\gamma
, and a (necessarily unique) solution to the system
(\gamma^{\ast}(E),\langle c_{0},e_{0}\rangle,\gamma_{\mathrm{H}})
exists. Then there exists a unique map
\hat{\gamma}\colon C\to E
such that:

\star

\hat{\gamma}
starts at
e_{0}
, that is,
c_{0}\hat{\gamma}=e_{0}
;

\star

\hat{\gamma}
is above
\gamma
, that is,
\hat{\gamma}q=\gamma
;

\star

\hat{\gamma}
is parallel
, that is, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{V}q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\hat{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}}

Proof.

Again, most of this follows similarly to the proof of Theorem 5.20 in
[
3
]
. The only difference is the proof and statement of the last condition. The proof is very similar to the proof in the middle of page 883 in that reference, except that

\mathrm{H}\mathsf{K}=\pi_{1}q0_{q}

in that proof is replaced by

\mathrm{H}\mathrm{R}=q^{\mathrm{F}}z_{q}^{\mathrm{V}}.

The result then follows after a few simple calculations.
∎
